```text
≠ retroactive uninterrupted realization

aborted segment
≠ aborted complete path automatically

realized prefix
≠ realized continuation automatically
```

The anti-laundering boundary is also preserved. A failed Path claim may retain valid configurations, transitions, chronology, duration, delay, local status, alternatives, evidence, and residue. The retained material does not prove the stronger Path claim. A later successful Trajectory reconstruction may formulate a new claim from new or revised evidence, but it cannot retroactively repair the earlier failed Path or remove its Failure Reason, Loss, and Re-entry Condition.

```text
preserved path material
≠ preserved stronger path claim

later trajectory success
≠ retroactive path repair
```

The same no-erasure rule applies when the analyst changes frame, granularity, relative level, selection rule, endpoint, comparison basis, label, or target function. The revised construction is a new testable claim. It must preserve lineage to the earlier result and cannot convert an omission, source gap, or failed traversal relation into continuity merely by redescribing the object.

The Path object remains distinct from the operations grammar. Chapter 10 specifies what must be true of the reconstructed historical object. Chapter 15 later specifies how selected source structures may be transformed through `COMPOSE`, including operation identity, formation mapping, loss, alternatives, and routing. A warranted Path may therefore be an input to a later operation, but it is neither a completed `COMPOSE` record nor automatic authorization for composition.

The chapter-level result is:

```text
admissible_but_provisional
```

The provisionality does not identify a known local defect. It preserves pressure from Chapter 11 Trajectory, Chapter 12 Path Dependence, Chapter 13 alternatives, Chapter 15 `COMPOSE`, Chapter 16 PATH Boundary Conditions, Chapter 17 cases and local audit, later Parts, LIMITS, Corpus Audit, and Model Finalization.

```text
Chapter 10 provisional lock
≠ historical truth proof
≠ trajectory preauthorization
≠ path-dependence confirmation
≠ COMPOSE authorization
≠ higher authority
```

The next controlled production step is:

```text
Chapter 11 — Preparation:
Trajectory
```


<a id="chapter-11-trajectory"></a>

## 11. Trajectory

Chapter 10 established the Path as the actually traversed, selectively reconstructed chain of relevant configurations and transitions within a declared frame. Chapter 11 asks a stronger but still bounded question: when does that traversed history remain structurally active in the present configuration and in the field of possible continuation?

A Path does not become a Trajectory because it is long, repeatedly described, internally coherent, costly, institutionally visible, or retrospectively important. The stronger object requires **sedimented historical load**: earlier Path structure must remain traceably carried into the present analytical cut through cumulative change, persistent residue, altered praxis conditions, or changed continuation possibilities.

```text
warranted Path
≠ Trajectory automatically
```

Trajectory is a derived temporal object within PMS-STRATA. It is not a new PMS operator, not a fourth STRATA operation, not a completed `COMPOSE` occurrence, not a causal law, not a target function, and not an authority-bearing historical verdict. The abstract operator type `Θ` supplies temporal structuring within PMS Base; it does not by itself establish a Trajectory occurrence. Likewise, recurrence, Asymmetry, Binding, or Non-Event occurrences may contribute to sedimentation, but their symbols do not substitute for an occurrence-level source trace.

The local work of Sections 11.1–11.4 is therefore to establish:

- the operational Trajectory definition;
- the conjunctive Path-to-Trajectory threshold;
- Historical Sedimentation as traceable retention and cumulative transformation;
- historical co-determination without exclusive causal determination;
- directionality without teleology;
- downgrade, Failure, and lineage rules where the stronger claim is not warranted.

The detailed operator-combination profiles belong to Sections 11.5–11.8, Changed Action Corridors to Section 11.9, Trajectory Boundary and Compression to Sections 11.10–11.11, competing constructions to Section 11.12, False Trajectory to Section 11.13, and the Minimal Trajectory Record to Section 11.14. Chapter 12 separately tests Path Dependence. Chapter 15 later owns the complete `COMPOSE` procedure. RETYPE later owns any contextual function assigned to a Trajectory.

<a id="11-1-definition-of-trajectory"></a>

### 11.1 Definition of Trajectory

A **Trajectory** is a temporally ordered Path whose source-supported repeated, cumulative, or irreversible structure has sedimented such that it historically co-determines the present configuration and bounded continuation possibilities.

```text
Trajectory
=
Path
+ sedimented historical load
+ present praxis relevance
```

This compact form is explanatory, not arithmetic. It does not authorize a score, a fixed duration threshold, an automatic classifier, or a mechanical conversion from Path fields. Each term carries an independent burden.

#### Operational definition

The Chapter-11 operational definition is:

```text
A Trajectory is a warranted Path
whose earlier configurations, transitions,
Non-Events, Asymmetries, Bindings,
Attractor relations, exclusions, or repairs
remain traceably carried into the present cut
through cumulative change, persistent residue,
and altered praxis or continuation conditions,
without implying teleology or Path Dependence automatically.
```

The definition preserves the Foundations definition while making its PATH burden explicit. It does not redefine configuration, transition, Path, sedimentation, irreversibility, or Path Dependence. It specifies what must be additionally shown after the Chapter-10 Path threshold has been met.

#### Trajectory is a historical object, not a stronger word for Path

A Path establishes actual traversal and constitutive connection. A Trajectory establishes that the traversed history has become a present-bearing historical structure.

```text
Path
→ what was actually traversed

Trajectory
→ how that traversal remains structurally operative
```

The difference is not merely one of descriptive detail. A richer Path description may add dates, actors, intermediate events, documents, or local transitions without establishing any retained historical load. Conversely, a comparatively short Path may qualify as a Trajectory where a threshold crossing, binding change, institutional inscription, irreversible cost, or persistent residue materially changes the present configuration.

```text
more Path detail
≠ more Trajectory warrant automatically
```

#### Conjunctive burden

A Trajectory candidate requires at least:

1. a warranted Path substrate or an explicitly bounded warranted Path segment;
2. a declared present analytical cut;
3. a repeated, cumulative, threshold, or irreversible contribution carried through the Path;
4. a continuity or carrier relation connecting earlier structure to the present cut;
5. persistent residue, cumulative transformation, or both;
6. a present praxis effect on meaning, cost, role, expectation, Asymmetry, Binding, accessibility, or continuation;
7. a source trace sufficient for the claimed relation;
8. counterpressure from repair, reversal, current conditions, rival construction, or uncertainty;
9. non-teleological directionality where direction is claimed;
10. a bounded source, claim, and authority ceiling.

```text
warranted Path
+ historical carrier
+ cumulative or sedimented relation
+ present praxis effect
+ source-result dependency
→ Trajectory candidate
```

A warranted Trajectory additionally requires the applicable STRATA checks for PraxisPurchase, TraceableLoad, Type Integrity, Reference Continuity, Temporal Continuity, Counterfactual Sensitivity, Contextual Boundedness, Loss, Stop, and Non-Capture.

```text
Trajectory candidate
≠ warranted Trajectory
```

No single burden compensates for another. Long duration does not compensate for missing present effect. A strong present effect does not compensate for missing Path lineage. A coherent narrative does not compensate for absent source trace. An operator-rich description does not compensate for Type Integrity failure.

#### Historical co-determination

The term **historically co-determines** states a bounded relation: the present configuration or continuation field cannot be reconstructed in the same warranted way if the relevant earlier Path structure is removed, materially altered, or assigned a different carrier relation.

```text
historical co-determination
≠ exclusive causation
≠ complete causal mechanism
≠ deterministic closure
```

The present may also be materially shaped by current rules, resources, actors, technologies, environments, interventions, or exogenous events. A Trajectory claim must therefore identify what the historical load contributes without treating history as the only active explanation.

Historical co-determination is also weaker than the Path-Dependence property tested in Chapter 12. Chapter 11 establishes retained historical load. Chapter 12 asks how strongly the present or future is dependent on the specific earlier order, branching, or historical route.

```text
Path burden
< Trajectory burden
< Path-Dependence burden
```

This is an ordering of claim load, not of truth, importance, or authority.

#### Object identity and origin-type preservation

A Trajectory remains a derived temporal object whose reference is carried by the underlying Path and its constitutive historical relations. It does not become a PMS primitive or an operator type. If it is later projected as a frame-function, macro-event, or attractor-function, its Trajectory origin type remains visible and the projection requires a separate `PROJECT_AS` record.

```text
Trajectory object
≠ Θ operator type
≠ Path-Dependence property
≠ target function
≠ authority grant
```

A changed boundary, Path substrate, present cut, frame, granularity, constitutive carrier, or target function creates a new testable claim. It does not silently overwrite the earlier Trajectory result.

#### C11-DEF-01 — warranted Path with cumulative Asymmetry, residue, and changed exit cost

A review process follows a warranted Path:

- `K0`: participation is voluntary and exit carries only ordinary administrative cost;
- `T1`: a provisional coordination rule assigns one role repeated after-hours responsibility;
- `K1`: the role continues while review is repeatedly deferred;
- `T2`: the temporary allocation is incorporated into standard procedure;
- `K2`: withdrawal now requires replacement coverage, approval, and loss of accumulated access.

The Path record establishes actual traversal, the relevant transitions, the repeated deferral, the selected continuation, and the open residue. Chapter 11 adds the following source-supported relations:

- the unequal burden is retained and cumulatively normalized;
- repeated review Non-Events leave unresolved procedural residue;
- the present exit cost is higher because replacement and approval requirements were built through the Path;
- removing `T1`, the deferrals, or the incorporation at `T2` would materially alter the warranted reconstruction of `K2`;
- alternative review and redistribution routes remained possible at earlier cuts and were not historically necessary.

The bounded result is a positive Trajectory candidate. It establishes neither Path Dependence, intent, legitimacy, permanence, nor a later frame-function.

```text
warranted Path
+ cumulative Asymmetry
+ persistent review residue
+ changed exit cost
→ bounded Trajectory candidate
```

The candidate becomes warranted only after the later Chapter-11 boundary, compression, competing-construction, record, and integrated admissibility duties are completed.

<a id="11-2-from-path-to-trajectory"></a>

### 11.2 From Path to Trajectory

The transition from Path to Trajectory is not an automatic upward movement in level, truth, or authority. It is a new claim about the retained structural load of an already warranted traversed history.

```text
new temporal object claim
=
new testable claim
```

Chapter 10 supplies the Path substrate. Chapter 11 tests whether the Path's earlier structure remains constitutive at the present cut. The source material may overlap, but the claim, burden, counterfactual, and Failure conditions differ.

#### Necessary Path substrate

A Trajectory claim must identify:

- the Path record or bounded Path segment used as source;
- its frame, temporal scope, granularity, relative level, and claim scope;
- its transition lineage and actual traversal basis;
- its selection, compression, alternatives, uncertainty, and loss;
- its endpoint or present analytical cut;
- any earlier Failure, Claim Reduction, or Non-Capture status.

A failed Path cannot become a valid Trajectory source merely because the later label is stronger. A revised Path can support a new Trajectory test only where the revision is explicit, source-supported, and linked to the earlier result.

```text
failed Path
+ stronger historical label
≠ repaired Path
```

#### Additional load beyond traversal

The Path-to-Trajectory test asks five linked questions:

1. **What was retained or transformed?**  
   Which earlier relation, burden, expectation, exclusion, Binding, Asymmetry, Attractor pattern, or Non-Event remains relevant?

2. **How was it carried?**  
   Through which configurations, transitions, records, routines, roles, costs, material arrangements, expectations, or repeated omissions did the historical load persist or change?

3. **What accumulated or sedimented?**  
   Did the Path produce cumulative change, threshold effects, persistent residue, altered local friction, non-restored costs, or changed accessibility?

4. **What differs at the present cut?**  
   Which current meaning, role, cost, expectation, option, exit condition, action corridor, or continuation would be reconstructed differently without that history?

5. **What remains uncertain or rivalrous?**  
   Which current-condition explanations, repairs, reversals, alternative periodizations, missing carriers, or competing constructions limit the claim?

A positive answer to only one question is insufficient. The test is relational and conjunctive.

#### Path-to-Trajectory threshold matrix

| Burden | Path can satisfy it? | Additional Trajectory requirement |
| --- | --- | --- |
| actual traversal | yes | the traversed structure remains present-bearing |
| temporal order | yes | order contributes to retained historical load |
| duration | yes | duration carries cumulative or sedimented effect |
| repetition | yes | repetition changes accessibility, cost, expectation, role, or residue |
| persistence | yes | the persistent relation is historically carried and praxis-relevant now |
| alternatives | yes | earlier selection or closure remains relevant to the present field |
| residue | yes | residue remains constitutive rather than merely archival or remembered |
| endpoint | yes | the present cut is shaped by the Path without being treated as a necessary goal |
| coherent narrative | possible | narrative coherence remains subordinate to source-result dependency |

The matrix is not a scoring system. A row cannot be counted as a point, and high coverage cannot compensate for a failed constitutive relation.

#### Present historical load and Source–Result Dependency

The core test is whether the result depends on the alleged historical carrier.

> Would a relevant change in the earlier Path structure alter the warranted reconstruction of the present configuration or bounded continuation possibilities?

Possible findings include:

- **sensitive:** the present reconstruction changes materially;
- **partially sensitive:** only specified dimensions change;
- **insensitive:** the Trajectory label remains unchanged despite relevant source variation;
- **underdetermined:** the source relation cannot responsibly be resolved;
- **not testable with available sources:** the required counterfactual trace is unavailable.

An insensitive result places the Trajectory claim under strong pressure. The analyst may still preserve a Path, duration, recurrence, persistence, or local historical association. The stronger claim requires reduction or Failure where the present result does not depend on the alleged historical load.

#### Current conditions and historical contribution

A current condition can reproduce, amplify, interrupt, or replace an earlier historical carrier. Chapter 11 must therefore distinguish at least:

```text
historical carry-over
current-condition production
interaction between both
underdetermined allocation
```

A present rule may continue a prior Path because it was historically installed, or it may have been independently introduced later. A current cost may be inherited, newly imposed, or jointly produced. The existence of a reconstructible Path does not settle the allocation.

Where current conditions fully account for the tested present relation and the earlier Path adds no source-supported discrimination, the Trajectory claim is not warranted for that dimension. Where historical and current conditions interact, the claim must remain explicitly co-determinative.

#### C11-PATH-01 — valid Path with weak present historical load

A team has followed the same monthly reporting sequence for six years. The Path is warranted: each report was prepared, reviewed, and archived through a stable chain. The present report, however, is generated from a newly introduced template and current data; earlier reports do not alter access, cost, role, expectation, or continuation beyond serving as optional examples. Removing the earlier sequence would not materially change the warranted reconstruction of the current reporting process.

The result preserves:

- the six-year Path;
- duration and recurrence;
- a stable reporting routine;
- the existence of archival continuity.

It does not establish sedimented historical load for the tested current process.

```text
valid Path
+ recurrence
+ weak present Source–Result Dependency
→ claim_reduction_required
```

The full Trajectory claim is reduced to the warranted Path and recurrence findings. This is not a claim that the history is unimportant in every possible frame; it is a bounded result for the declared present-process claim.

#### C11-DUR-01 — long-duration Path without sedimentation

A permit renewal follows a documented twenty-year Path. Each renewal is legally and operationally independent: no previous renewal changes the current evidentiary burden, fee, access, review corridor, role distribution, or future option. Earlier files are retained only because of a general archive rule and are not consulted in the current decision.

The duration is real and the Path is warranted. Yet:

- the same label does not establish carrier continuity;
- archival survival does not establish persistent residue;
- repetition does not alter local friction or continuation;
- no relevant counterfactual change in earlier renewals alters the present reconstruction.

The correct result is:

```text
long-duration Path
≠ Trajectory
```

Duration and repeated occurrence are preserved. The Trajectory claim fails for the declared object and present cut. A different claim—such as institutional memory carried by archived precedent—would require new evidence and a separate test.

#### Downgrade and no-erasure rule

Failure to establish Trajectory does not erase a warranted Path or valid local findings.

```text
failed Trajectory
→ preserve warranted Path
→ preserve duration, recurrence, persistence, residue, or local accumulation where supported
→ record Failure reason and re-entry condition
```

Re-entry may occur through:

- new source material identifying a carrier;
- a revised Path lineage;
- a narrower present-effect claim;
- a changed analytical cut;
- an explicitly different frame or granularity.

Re-entry does not occur merely by adding more dates, extending the period, choosing a more dramatic endpoint, or changing the label.

<a id="11-3-historical-sedimentation"></a>

### 11.3 Historical Sedimentation

**Historical Sedimentation** is the traceable retention and cumulative transformation of earlier Path structure such that the retained or transformed load remains materially relevant to the present configuration or bounded continuation possibilities.

```text
Historical Sedimentation
=
source-supported historical carrier
+ cumulative or persistent transformation
+ present praxis effect
```

Sedimentation is neither a metaphor for age nor a claim that history accumulates like uniform material. It identifies a relation in which earlier structure remains active through carriers, residues, costs, expectations, roles, procedures, exclusions, or altered accessibility.

#### Sedimentation is not elapsed time

Elapsed time can permit sedimentation, but does not prove it.

```text
duration
≠ sedimentation

repetition
≠ sedimentation

persistence
≠ historical load automatically
```

A structure may persist unchanged because current conditions recreate it independently at each occurrence. A repeated event may leave no cumulative trace. A long interval may contain repair, replacement, or discontinuity. Conversely, a single threshold transition may sediment quickly where it irreversibly changes access, cost, role, or institutional procedure.

The question is not how much time passed, but what earlier structure remains carried and what difference that carry-over makes now.

#### Carrier relation

A sedimentation claim must identify how historical load is carried. Possible carriers include:

- procedural rules or institutional records;
- stabilized role allocations;
- material infrastructure;
- sunk coordination or reopening costs;
- repeated expectations and default assumptions;
- access rights or exclusions;
- unresolved commitments;
- altered information distribution;
- recurrent low-friction continuations;
- durable residue from framed Non-Events;
- translated carriers across a declared frame or role change.

The same name, institution, person, category, or document series does not prove carrier continuity. Where the carrier changes, the translation relation must be explicit.

```text
same label
≠ same carrier
```

#### Cumulative change

Cumulative change is a temporally distributed transformation whose present effect depends on more than one isolated occurrence or on a threshold produced through the Path. It may take several forms:

- repeated reinforcement;
- threshold crossing;
- incremental narrowing or widening of access;
- increased breach, exit, or repair cost;
- partial erosion with retained residue;
- alternating movement that changes the range of reachable options;
- distributed contributions across several carriers;
- repair that restores one dimension while leaving another altered.

```text
cumulative change
≠ monotonic increase
≠ simple numerical addition
```

A Trajectory may therefore contain reversals, interruptions, and repairs. The issue is whether the historical load survives, is translated, is reduced, or is dissolved—not whether every segment points in one direction.

#### Persistent residue

Persistent residue is unresolved or retained historical load that remains beyond the event, transition, or interval in which it arose. Examples include:

- an unresolved commitment;
- a non-restored cost distribution;
- institutional memory embedded in procedure;
- a previously available alternative that is now harder to recover;
- altered expectations after repeated deferral;
- reopening costs after partial repair;
- a Binding that remains dormant but can be reactivated;
- a material or documentary trace that changes present action.

```text
persistent residue
≠ permanence
≠ every surviving trace
≠ missing information
```

A residue may weaken, be redistributed, repaired, become dormant, or cease to be constitutive. Its later absence cannot be assumed from silence, and its continuing relevance cannot be inferred merely from archival survival.

#### Changed present praxis conditions

Sedimentation must produce a bounded difference in current praxis. The difference may concern:

- action corridors;
- exit, breach, repair, or reconsideration cost;
- role and responsibility distribution;
- exposure or access;
- expected continuation;
- visibility or practical availability of alternatives;
- the meaning of a current event or Non-Event;
- the resources required to reverse or redirect the Path.

```text
historical interest
≠ present praxis effect
```

A historically rich account can be accurate and still fail the Trajectory threshold if the tested present relation does not depend on that history.

#### Sedimentation matrix

| Dimension | Required declaration | Failure pressure |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Path substrate | warranted traversal and selection lineage | chronology or failed Path is hidden |
| sedimented structure | what is retained, transformed, or accumulated | generic statement that “history matters” |
| carrier | how the load persists or is translated | name or label continuity substitutes for relation |
| cumulative contribution | repetition, threshold, irreversible change, erosion, or repair | duration or event count substitutes for accumulation |
| persistent residue | what remains at the present cut | permanence is assumed or missing information is converted into residue |
| present praxis effect | what meaning, cost, role, expectation, access, or continuation differs | historical background is treated as present load |
| counterpressure | repair, reversal, current conditions, rival carrier, uncertainty | narrative coherence immunizes the claim |
| source/claim ceiling | strongest warranted historical relation | detail volume or formal completeness increases authority |

The dimensions are non-compensatory. A detailed carrier with no present effect does not establish sedimentation. A clear present effect with no traceable historical carrier does not establish sedimentation. A complete record does not decide substantive sufficiency.

#### Repair, erosion, interruption, and dormancy

Sedimentation is compatible with change in strength and form. A repair may:

- remove the relevant residue and dissolve the Trajectory claim;
- reduce the claim to an earlier segment;
- restore access while leaving reopening cost;
- redistribute Asymmetry without removing it;
- interrupt one carrier while another remains;
- create a new Trajectory segment with explicit lineage.

An interruption does not automatically erase prior load. Dormancy does not automatically preserve it. The record must show the present relation at the declared cut.

```text
repair
≠ reset automatically

interruption
≠ erasure automatically

dormant trace
≠ present constitutive load automatically
```

#### Counterfactual sedimentation test

The core counterfactual is:

> If the alleged historical carrier or cumulative contribution were materially different, would the present praxis reconstruction change?

The test must be bounded. It does not require a complete alternate history. It requires enough source-supported variation to identify whether the claimed present relation is sensitive to the alleged historical load.

Where the counterfactual cannot be responsibly tested, a Formal Diagnostic or `admissible_but_provisional` result may preserve the open relation. Where the result is insensitive, Claim Reduction or Failure is required. Where rival source-responsible carrier relations remain non-decidable, later Non-Capture may be appropriate.

#### C11-SED-01 — non-linear accumulation with repair and remaining residue

A service organization develops a Path in which repeated access exceptions are granted informally, later restricted after a failure, and then partially repaired through a new review procedure.

The reconstruction shows:

- early exceptions lowered friction for one role and raised uncertainty for another;
- a later restriction reversed the direction of access but retained unequal information and reopening cost;
- the repair restored formal review yet required evidence that only long-standing participants possessed;
- the present corridor is neither the original open corridor nor the restrictive corridor;
- removing the earlier exception records and restriction would alter the warranted explanation of present access and proof burden.

The result supports a bounded sedimentation claim:

```text
reinforcement
→ restriction
→ partial repair
→ remaining information and reopening residue
```

The Trajectory is non-linear. Repair is visible and materially effective, but it does not constitute a reset. The claim is limited to access, information, and reopening cost. It does not establish an overall direction of institutional progress or decline, strong Path Dependence, or legitimacy.

<a id="11-4-directionality-without-teleology"></a>

### 11.4 Directionality without Teleology

A Trajectory may have **directionality** where successive sedimented relations alter specified continuation conditions in a recurrent or cumulative orientation. Directionality is a bounded pattern in the historical object. It is not a claim that the Path was aimed at, destined for, or necessarily completed by its current endpoint.

```text
directionality
≠ teleology
```

#### Dimension-specific direction

Direction must be stated by dimension. Examples include:

- rising exit cost;
- narrowing reconsideration routes;
- increasing formalization;
- reduced visibility of alternatives;
- stronger or weaker Binding;
- redistributed Asymmetry;
- growing or declining residual load;
- locally easier recurrence of one continuation.

A single Trajectory may move in different directions across different dimensions.

```text
higher formal stability
+ lower practical accessibility
+ reduced one-sided burden
≠ one total direction
```

Therefore Chapter 11 prohibits undifferentiated labels such as inevitable progress, decline, maturation, collapse, optimization, or radicalization unless the label is itself decomposed into source-supported dimensions and bounded claims. Even then, the label does not acquire teleological force.

#### Directionality is compatible with variation

Internal variation, interruption, reversal, and repair do not automatically invalidate a directional claim. They may:

- weaken the direction;
- delimit it to one segment;
- reverse it after a threshold;
- leave residue despite reversal;
- produce competing periodizations;
- dissolve the claim entirely.

The analyst must distinguish:

```text
segment direction
complete-Trajectory direction
present continuation pressure
```

A directional early segment does not establish the direction of the complete Trajectory. A present continuation pressure does not prove that earlier actors intended the endpoint. A reversal does not become proof that no earlier directional accumulation existed.

#### Anti-teleology requirements

A warranted directionality claim must preserve:

1. source-supported unrealized alternatives;
2. branch points and non-selection where relevant;
3. contingencies, interruptions, reversals, repairs, and exogenous events;
4. uncertainty about intention, foresight, and planning;
5. the difference between a later coherent account and an earlier operative plan;
6. the distinction between increased accessibility, probability, and necessity;
7. the possibility that the present endpoint was not anticipated;
8. counterevidence and competing periodizations;
9. a claim-specific source and temporal boundary.

```text
retrospective coherence
≠ original plan

recurrent continuation
≠ destiny

increased local likelihood
≠ necessity
```

A source may document an intention or plan. That supports an intention or plan claim for the relevant actor and time. It does not prove that the complete Trajectory was caused by, faithful to, or destined to realize that plan.

#### Endpoint-selection bias

Teleological error often enters when the current endpoint determines which earlier events are selected as meaningful. The analyst then removes reversals, failed branches, unrelated current conditions, or countervailing evidence until the history appears to lead naturally to the endpoint.

A responsible Trajectory construction must ask:

- Would these transitions still be selected if the endpoint were different?
- Are earlier events load-bearing because sources show a carrier, or because the endpoint makes them narratively attractive?
- Which alternatives were genuinely available at the time?
- Which later categories are being projected backward?
- Does the directional claim survive inclusion of repair, reversal, and countertrend?

```text
endpoint-selected history
≠ source-traceable Trajectory automatically
```

#### Direction and bounded continuation possibilities

A directional Trajectory changes the present field without eliminating agency or contingency. It may make one continuation easier, cheaper, more visible, institutionally normal, or expected, while making another harder, costlier, less visible, or dependent on repair.

```text
directional continuation pressure
≠ determined future
```

The future remains open to new events, Non-Events, recontextualization, distance, integration, Binding changes, external intervention, or structural break. Chapter 11 records the historically altered field, not a prediction.

#### C11-DIR-01 — dimension-specific direction with visible alternatives

A policy Path moves through informal guidance, repeated adoption, procedural codification, and a later review challenge. Across the Path:

- compliance becomes progressively easier because forms and routines are standardized;
- exit from the procedure becomes costlier because alternative routes lose staff and documentation;
- contestation remains formally available and is used in the review challenge;
- one partial reversal restores an appeal route but does not restore the former informal flexibility.

The warranted directional claim is limited:

```text
increasing procedural stabilization
+ increasing cost of non-standard continuation
+ preserved but narrowed challenge route
```

The construction preserves the challenge, the partial reversal, and earlier alternatives. It does not claim that codification was inevitable, that the policy progressed, that all actors shared a plan, or that later continuation is determined.

The result is a bounded non-teleological directionality finding within a Trajectory candidate.

#### C11-TEL-01 — endpoint-driven story implying destiny

A retrospective report begins with the current centralized procedure and selects only earlier decisions that can be presented as steps toward centralization. It omits:

- two documented decentralization attempts;
- a prolonged period in which both models coexisted;
- an external funding change that made centralization newly affordable;
- a review Non-Event that left the decentralized route unsupported;
- evidence that early participants expected the arrangement to remain temporary.

The report concludes that the organization had “always been moving toward” centralization.

The chronology may be partially correct, and a bounded Path may remain reconstructible. The directional conclusion is teleological because the endpoint controls selection, alternatives are removed, later categories are projected backward, and the external condition is suppressed.

The required disposition is:

```text
teleological Trajectory claim
→ claim_reduction_required or failed_transformation
```

The analyst may retain:

- supported configurations and transitions;
- the later centralizing segment;
- the external funding change;
- the review Non-Event;
- competing periodizations;
- uncertainty about the complete direction.

The stronger destiny or original-plan claim is not preserved.

#### Chapter-12 boundary

Directionality does not establish Path Dependence. A Trajectory can show historical orientation and present load while current conditions still permit several continuations at comparable cost. Chapter 12 separately tests whether and how the specific historical route constrains, channels, or determines the present and future beyond what current conditions alone explain.

```text
non-teleological directionality
≠ weak Path Dependence automatically
≠ strong Path Dependence automatically
```

#### WP1 completion boundary

Sections 11.1–11.4 establish the operational Trajectory definition, the Path-to-Trajectory threshold, Historical Sedimentation, historical co-determination, and directionality without teleology. They realize the six assigned WP1 pressure cases:

```text
C11-DEF-01
C11-PATH-01
C11-DUR-01
C11-SED-01
C11-DIR-01
C11-TEL-01
```

WP1 does not yet establish the complete Chapter-11 object. It does not operationalize the four operator-combination profiles in full, complete Changed Action Corridors, fix Trajectory Boundary or Compression, adjudicate competing constructions, define False Trajectory comprehensively, produce the Minimal Trajectory Record, or provisionally lock Chapter 11.

The local result is:

```text
admissible_but_provisional
```

The provisionality identifies no known WP1 defect. It preserves pressure from Sections 11.5–11.14, Chapter 12 Path Dependence, Chapter 15 `COMPOSE`, later Parts, cases, appendices, LIMITS, Corpus Audit, and Model Finalization.

```text
Chapter 11 WP1 completion
≠ Chapter 11 provisional lock
≠ Path-Dependence confirmation
≠ COMPOSE authorization
≠ target-function assignment
≠ higher authority
```

The next controlled production step is:

```text
Chapter 11 — WP2:
Attractor Sedimentation,
Asymmetry Accumulation,
Binding Accumulation,
Residual Accumulation,
and Changed Action Corridors
```

Sections **11.5–11.9**.
<a id="11-5-attractor-sedimentation"></a>

### 11.5 Attractor Sedimentation

**Attractor Sedimentation** is the historically traceable stabilization of an Attractor-typed occurrence across a Path such that a specified continuation becomes locally easier, more expected, more visible, or more readily coordinated at the present analytical cut.

It is an occurrence-level relation between bounded `Α` structures and temporal development under `Θ`. It is not a fused operator, a mechanical formula, or a new primitive.

```text
operator combination
≠ operator fusion
```

```text
Α occurrence
+ temporal trace under Θ
+ changed continuation accessibility
≠ new operator
≠ Trajectory automatically
```

Attractor Sedimentation may contribute to a Trajectory where repeated configurations do more than recur. The Path must show how recurrence modifies the practical field in which later action occurs.

#### Repetition and stabilized continuation

Repetition is relevant where it leaves a carrier. Possible carriers include:

- routines that reduce coordination effort;
- forms, procedures, interfaces, or infrastructures that preselect a continuation;
- repeated role expectations that make one response locally normal;
- learned sequencing that shortens decision time;
- recurrent allocation of attention or resources;
- repeated Non-Events that leave an alternative unpracticed or unsupported;
- records and precedents that make one continuation easier to justify;
- material arrangements that lower the friction of recurrence.

```text
repeated configuration
≠ Attractor Sedimentation automatically
```

A configuration may recur many times without becoming historically load-bearing. Attractor Sedimentation requires a source-supported relation between the recurrence and a changed present field. The relevant change may concern local friction, default accessibility, expectation, visibility, coordination cost, or the practical availability of alternatives.

#### Local friction and default accessibility

Local friction is the effort, delay, uncertainty, authorization burden, coordination work, or material cost required to enact a continuation within the declared frame.

An Attractor may sediment where the Path:

- makes one route easier to initiate;
- makes one sequence easier to complete;
- supplies familiar roles and expectations;
- provides reusable resources or precedents;
- decreases the need for renewed negotiation;
- increases the cost of deviating from the established continuation;
- lets an alternative remain formally available while becoming practically obscure.

```text
lower local friction
≠ rational superiority
≠ legitimacy
≠ necessity
```

The easiest continuation is not thereby the best, correct, authorized, or inevitable continuation. Chapter 11 reconstructs historical stabilization, not normative rank.

#### Attractor continuity and translation

Attractor Sedimentation does not require identical surface repetition. The carrier may be translated across changed roles, technologies, procedures, or frames. A formerly informal routine may become a formal workflow; a repeated conversational expectation may be embedded in a template; a local workaround may become an institutional default.

A translation claim must identify:

1. the earlier Attractor-typed occurrence;
2. the later carrier or translated form;
3. the constitutive relation preserved across the change;
4. the altered local friction or continuation accessibility;
5. discontinuities, repairs, reversals, and rival explanations;
6. the source and claim ceiling.

```text
same recurrence label
≠ Attractor continuity

changed surface form
≠ Attractor discontinuity automatically
```

Nominal continuity is insufficient, but surface change does not erase a supported carrier.

#### Reinforcement, erosion, and interruption

Attractor Sedimentation may strengthen, weaken, fragment, or become conditionally dormant. Relevant developments include:

- repeated reinforcement;
- partial erosion after a failed continuation;
- deliberate interruption through new review requirements;
- reactivation when an earlier routine remains easier to restore than an alternative;
- coexistence of competing attractors;
- local stabilization in one segment and dissolution in another;
- repair that preserves infrastructure while changing its use.

A later interruption may reduce the present Attractor load without proving that no sedimentation occurred. Conversely, historical recurrence does not establish present Attractor load where current infrastructure, roles, or incentives have replaced the earlier carrier.

```text
past Attractor Sedimentation
≠ current dominance automatically

current recurrence
≠ historically sedimented recurrence automatically
```

The record must separate historical carry-over, current-condition production, and their interaction.

#### Profile-specific burden

A bounded Attractor Sedimentation claim should declare:

```yaml
attractor_sedimentation:
  path_segment:
  attractor_occurrence:
  recurring_configurations:
  historical_carrier:
  changed_local_friction:
  changed_default_accessibility:
  alternatives_affected:
  repair_or_erosion:
  current_condition_contribution:
  source_trace:
  counterfactual_pressure:
  claim_scope:
```

This is a chapter-level declaration pattern, not a new formal schema field. The Shared Transformation Record remains the governing record architecture.

#### Cross-profile boundary

Attractor Sedimentation is distinct from the other WP2 profiles:

```text
Attractor Sedimentation
≠ Asymmetry Accumulation
≠ Binding Accumulation
≠ Residual Accumulation
```

The same Path segment may contribute to several profiles. A routine can lower local friction, distribute costs unequally, reinforce commitments, and leave unresolved expectations. Each claim nevertheless requires its own source trace and present effect.

A rich Attractor profile cannot compensate for:

- missing persistent residue where residue is constitutive to the Trajectory claim;
- absent changed present praxis conditions;
- failed Reference or Temporal Continuity;
- unsupported Asymmetry or Binding claims;
- a failed Path substrate;
- missing Source–Result Dependency.

#### C11-ATTR-01 — repeated low-friction continuation under `Α + Θ`

A service team initially handles exceptional requests through ad hoc discussion. Over several cycles, one sequence is repeatedly used:

1. a requester submits a short informal note;
2. one coordinator routes the note to the same reviewer;
3. the reviewer approves by copying a prior response;
4. no alternative review route is documented;
5. new staff learn the sequence by observing earlier cases.

Later, a shared template and routing shortcut are created. The formal policy still permits several routes, but the repeated sequence now:

- requires less coordination time;
- is more visible to staff;
- has reusable wording and precedent;
- reaches the familiar reviewer more quickly;
- leaves alternative routes formally open but practically underdeveloped.

The bounded result is:

```text
recurrent configuration
+ retained routing and precedent carrier
+ lower present local friction
+ reduced visibility of alternatives
→ bounded Attractor Sedimentation
```

The claim remains limited. It does not establish that the route is optimal, legitimate, necessary, or strongly Path Dependent. If current management later mandates the route independently of the earlier sequence, the analysis must separate present rule pressure from historical Attractor load.

#### Chapter-12 and RETYPE boundaries

Attractor Sedimentation does not establish the degree to which the present depends on the historical route. Chapter 12 separately tests whether alternative histories would produce materially different present accessibility beyond current conditions.

Nor does the Trajectory become an **attractor-function** in another context. Such a target-function claim belongs to a separately declared `PROJECT_AS` occurrence in RETYPE.

```text
Attractor Sedimentation within a Trajectory
≠ strong Path Dependence automatically
≠ Trajectory PROJECT_AS attractor-function
```

<a id="11-6-asymmetry-accumulation"></a>

### 11.6 Asymmetry Accumulation

**Asymmetry Accumulation** is the repeated retention, intensification, redistribution, or hardening of an Asymmetry-typed relation across a Path such that differential cost, access, exposure, capacity, information, responsibility burden, or exit condition remains materially relevant at the present analytical cut.

It is an occurrence-level relation between bounded `Ω` structures and temporal development under `Θ`.

```text
Ω occurrence
+ temporal distribution trace under Θ
+ present differential effect
≠ new operator
≠ legitimacy claim
```

#### Unequal outcome versus accumulated Asymmetry

A single unequal outcome may instantiate an Asymmetry occurrence. It does not by itself establish accumulation.

```text
unequal outcome
≠ Asymmetry Accumulation automatically
```

Accumulation requires a temporal carrier showing how differential load is retained, repeated, intensified, translated, or made harder to reverse. Relevant dimensions include:

- unequal access to information or decision points;
- repeated exposure to delay, uncertainty, or risk;
- differential ability to initiate, revise, or stop a process;
- unequal coordination or documentation burden;
- uneven access to repair, appeal, or reopening;
- recurring allocation of time, attention, or material cost;
- asymmetrical reputational or relational exposure;
- unequal practical exit conditions.

The dimensions must be declared. “Increasing inequality” is too broad where the record supports only one cost or access relation.

#### Temporal carriers of Asymmetry

Asymmetry may accumulate through:

- repeated decisions that allocate the same burden to one role;
- records or precedents that make one party repeatedly responsible for proof;
- infrastructure that gives one position earlier or better access;
- sunk costs that reduce one party’s exit capacity;
- repeated delay that exposes one role to escalating uncertainty;
- role specialization that concentrates knowledge and decision capacity;
- unreviewed exceptions that become accessible only to insiders;
- partial repair that redistributes one burden while leaving another intact.

```text
same roles over time
≠ accumulated Asymmetry automatically

role change
≠ erased Asymmetry automatically
```

A role label may persist while the relevant cost relation changes. Conversely, different individuals may occupy a role while the Asymmetry carrier remains institutional, procedural, or material.

#### Role hardening

A role hardens where repeated allocation makes a position increasingly difficult to leave, contest, reinterpret, or perform differently. Hardening may involve:

- accumulated expertise that becomes indispensable;
- repeated responsibility assignment without equivalent authority;
- expectations that one role will absorb coordination failure;
- documentation burdens that become prerequisites for exit or appeal;
- repeated access advantages that become self-reinforcing;
- reputational expectations that narrow permissible conduct.

Role hardening is a structural claim. It is not a personality attribution, diagnosis, moral ranking, or claim that the role-holder endorses the arrangement.

#### Exit conditions

Asymmetry Accumulation often becomes visible in changed exit conditions. Exit may remain formally available while practical exit costs rise through:

- lost investment;
- loss of access or status;
- unrecoverable coordination work;
- replacement or retraining burden;
- exposure of one role to disproportionate consequences;
- dependence on records controlled elsewhere;
- uncertainty about whether prior obligations survive exit.

```text
formal exit availability
≠ equal practical exit

high exit cost
≠ impossible exit
```

The claim must preserve remaining agency, available repair, and the difference between burden and impossibility.

#### Accumulation, redistribution, and repair

Asymmetry need not increase monotonically. A Trajectory may show:

- intensification in one period;
- redistribution after reform;
- partial repair of access but persistence of proof burden;
- reduced exposure alongside increased dependency;
- reversal for one role and new burden for another;
- dissolution of the historical carrier;
- competing Asymmetry profiles under different periodizations.

```text
repair
≠ reset automatically

remaining difference
≠ failed repair automatically
```

The analyst must state what changed, what remained, and whether the current Asymmetry is historically carried, currently produced, or jointly maintained.

#### Current-condition allocation

A present Asymmetry may be explained by current law, technology, resource scarcity, role design, or external events rather than by the reconstructed Path. Chapter 11 therefore asks:

- Would the present differential relation remain materially similar if the earlier Path were altered?
- Which current conditions independently reproduce it?
- Does the historical carrier explain distribution, magnitude, visibility, or reversibility?
- Are historical and current contributions distinguishable with available sources?

Where allocation remains unresolved, the result may be `admissible_but_provisional`, `partially_admissible`, `claim_reduction_required`, or `non_capture`, depending on the broader record.

#### Profile-specific burden

A bounded Asymmetry Accumulation claim should declare:

```yaml
asymmetry_accumulation:
  path_segment:
  asymmetry_occurrence:
  distribution_dimensions:
  historical_carrier:
  repeated_or_cumulative_load:
  present_distribution:
  exit_conditions:
  repair_or_redistribution:
  current_condition_contribution:
  source_trace:
  counterfactual_pressure:
  claim_scope:
```

This declaration does not authorize moral judgment, legitimacy assessment, sanction, person ranking, or automatic responsibility assignment.

#### C11-ASYM-01 — repeated unequal cost and exit burden under `Ω + Θ`

A collaborative program begins with two partner roles having comparable formal authority. Across successive funding cycles:

- one partner repeatedly provides emergency labor when deadlines slip;
- the other controls the reporting system and retains the complete records;
- reimbursement requires documents held by the reporting partner;
- each renewal makes the emergency role more specialized and harder to replace;
- a later reform equalizes meeting votes but leaves documentation access and replacement cost unchanged.

The bounded reconstruction identifies:

```text
repeated emergency burden
+ retained information control
+ specialized sunk coordination
+ partial governance repair
→ accumulated Asymmetry in labor, information, and exit cost
```

The result does not claim that one partner is morally superior, that the arrangement is illegitimate, that exit is impossible, or that every present difference is historically caused. The vote reform is materially recognized; the remaining documentation and replacement burdens support a narrower accumulation claim.

#### Chapter-12 boundary

Asymmetry Accumulation supplies a historical load profile. It does not yet establish how strongly the present configuration depends on the actual route rather than current distribution alone. Chapter 12 owns that graded dependence test.

```text
accumulated Asymmetry
≠ strong Path Dependence automatically
≠ deterministic role closure
```

<a id="11-7-binding-accumulation"></a>

### 11.7 Binding Accumulation

**Binding Accumulation** is the historically traceable strengthening, layering, transfer, conflict, or persistence of Binding-typed occurrences across a Path such that commitments, coordination investments, obligations, expectations, breach costs, or reopening conditions shape the present configuration or bounded continuation possibilities.

It is an occurrence-level relation between bounded `Ψ` structures and temporal development under `Θ`.

```text
Ψ occurrence
+ temporal commitment trace under Θ
+ present binding effect
≠ new operator
≠ moral obligation automatically
```

#### Structural Binding

Binding in Chapter 11 is structural. It may be carried by:

- explicit commitments;
- repeated assurances;
- contracts or procedural obligations;
- coordinated investments made in reliance on continuation;
- shared records or standards;
- role expectations stabilized through practice;
- dependencies created by specialization;
- commitments to review, repair, or return;
- costs attached to breach, withdrawal, reopening, or re-entry.

A structural Binding claim does not establish a person’s inner state, sincerity, identity, virtue, guilt, or moral duty.

```text
structural Binding
≠ psychological commitment
≠ moral obligation
≠ legitimate obligation automatically
```

#### Multiple commitments versus accumulated Binding

A series of commitments may remain discrete. Binding Accumulation requires a carrier through which later commitments inherit, layer, transform, or increase the practical consequences of earlier ones.

```text
multiple commitments
≠ Binding Accumulation automatically
```

Accumulation may occur where:

- each commitment presupposes the prior arrangement;
- coordinated investment becomes difficult to recover;
- repeated continuation raises expected follow-through;
- procedures make breach progressively more costly;
- a commitment is translated into rules, records, or infrastructure;
- unresolved earlier commitments condition later negotiation;
- exit requires reopening several linked arrangements rather than ending one event.

The claim must identify whether the Binding is cumulative, serial, nested, transferred, conflicting, or merely repeated.

#### Binding carriers

Historical carriers may include:

- signed agreements and amendments;
- shared plans and schedules;
- resource allocations that presuppose future continuation;
- institutional roles created to maintain the arrangement;
- records of promised review or repair;
- technical dependencies and interoperability commitments;
- repeated reliance by other roles;
- sunk coordination and switching costs.

```text
recorded promise
≠ present Binding automatically

continued coordination
≠ consensual endorsement automatically
```

A commitment may expire, be superseded, become unenforceable, be rejected, or persist only as residue. The record must establish current structural relevance rather than infer it from archival presence.

#### Strengthening, weakening, transfer, conflict, and dormancy

Binding may develop in several ways:

- **strengthening:** breach or reopening becomes costlier;
- **weakening:** later revisions reduce scope or consequences;
- **transfer:** Binding moves from persons to roles, procedures, or systems;
- **conflict:** several commitments become mutually difficult to satisfy;
- **fragmentation:** one composite Binding separates into local obligations;
- **dormancy:** a commitment is inactive but remains capable of reactivation;
- **release:** a supported transition removes the relevant Binding;
- **repair:** breach is addressed without restoring the prior structure exactly.

```text
weakened Binding
≠ absent historical Binding

historical Binding
≠ present enforceability automatically
```

Internal conflict does not automatically erase Binding Accumulation. It may instead be part of the historical load by increasing negotiation, breach, or reopening costs.

#### Breach and reopening cost

A central present effect of Binding Accumulation is the cost of changing course. Relevant costs may include:

- renegotiation across multiple roles;
- compensation or replacement;
- loss of interoperability;
- renewed authorization;
- restoration of trust-dependent coordination;
- reopening archived decisions;
- reallocation of sunk resources;
- clarification of surviving commitments.

High breach cost does not mean that continuation is required. Chapter 11 records the altered corridor; it does not issue an instruction.

```text
high breach cost
≠ mandatory continuation
≠ prohibition of exit
```

#### Profile-specific burden

A bounded Binding Accumulation claim should declare:

```yaml
binding_accumulation:
  path_segment:
  binding_occurrences:
  binding_relation:
  historical_carrier:
  cumulative_or_layered_commitments:
  reliance_or_coordination_investment:
  breach_or_reopening_cost:
  weakening_transfer_or_release:
  conflicting_bindings:
  present_effect:
  source_trace:
  counterfactual_pressure:
  claim_scope:
```

This remains a chapter-level declaration pattern within the Shared Transformation Record.

#### Cross-profile boundary

Binding Accumulation can interact with Attractor Sedimentation, Asymmetry Accumulation, and Residual Accumulation. For example, repeated coordination may become easier while burden is distributed unequally and an unfulfilled review commitment remains open.

The profiles remain non-substitutable:

```text
strong Binding profile
≠ proof of Attractor Sedimentation
≠ proof of accumulated Asymmetry
≠ proof of residual Non-Event load
```

Nor may Binding language be used to convert dependence, expectation, or repeated participation into consent, legitimacy, or person-level commitment.

#### C11-BIND-01 — commitments and breach costs under `Ψ + Θ`

Three organizations begin a temporary shared-data project. The initial agreement is narrow. Over four renewals:

- each organization adapts its workflow to the shared format;
- one joint archive becomes necessary for ongoing reporting;
- staff roles are created around cross-system coordination;
- each renewal promises a later portability review;
- the portability review is repeatedly deferred;
- ending the arrangement now requires data conversion, role reassignment, and clarification of surviving reporting commitments.

The bounded result is:

```text
serial renewals
+ cumulative workflow reliance
+ shared archive carrier
+ deferred portability review
+ increased conversion and reopening cost
→ bounded Binding Accumulation
```

The result does not establish a moral duty to continue, valid consent to every renewal, legal enforceability, or irreversible lock-in. The deferred review also contributes separately to Residual Accumulation; it is not absorbed into the Binding profile.

#### Chapter-12 boundary

Binding Accumulation may make the actual history relevant to current breach and reopening costs. Chapter 12 separately tests the degree and form of Path Dependence. Chapter 11 does not infer inevitability or necessity from accumulated commitments.

<a id="11-8-residual-accumulation"></a>

### 11.8 Residual Accumulation

**Residual Accumulation** is the historically traceable retention, layering, or transformation of expectation-bound Non-Event occurrences across a Path such that unresolved absence, delayed review, non-decision, blocked repair, excluded alternative, or unrealized transition remains materially relevant at the present analytical cut.

It is an occurrence-level relation between bounded `Λ` structures and temporal development under `Θ`.

```text
Λ occurrence
+ expectation and frame
+ temporal residue carrier under Θ
+ present effect
≠ new operator
≠ missing information
```

#### Non-Event identity remains frame-bound

Every contributing Non-Event must remain tied to a warranted expectation structure. The record must identify:

- what was expected;
- by whom or by which procedure, role, or institution;
- within which frame;
- during which temporal window;
- what did not occur;
- why the expectation was warranted;
- which residue persisted;
- how the present field differs.

```text
absence
≠ Non-Event automatically

missing information
≠ Non-Event

later disappointment
≠ earlier warranted expectation automatically
```

Residual Accumulation cannot be built by converting every gap, silence, undocumented interval, or unavailable source into `Λ`.

#### Forms of residual accumulation

Residual load may accumulate through:

- repeated deferral of a decision;
- an expected review that never occurs;
- successive repair commitments that remain unrealized;
- repeated non-response under a stable response expectation;
- unresolved responsibility assignment;
- an aborted transition whose costs or expectations remain;
- alternatives that were available but become inaccessible during delay;
- recurring exceptions never integrated into a revised rule;
- exclusions that persist because no reopening event occurs.

Accumulation is not merely a count of absences. It may involve:

- **layering:** several unresolved Non-Events remain simultaneously active;
- **extension:** one Non-Event persists beyond its expected window;
- **translation:** an unresolved expectation moves into a new procedure or role;
- **amplification:** later Non-Events increase the cost or meaning of earlier ones;
- **closure:** a later event resolves part or all of the residue;
- **dormancy:** residue becomes inactive but remains conditionally relevant;
- **redistribution:** unresolved load shifts across roles or contexts.

#### Residual carriers

Possible carriers include:

- open action items or unresolved records;
- unreviewed exceptions;
- continuing expectations among roles;
- procedures built around an anticipated but absent decision;
- sunk preparation for a transition that did not occur;
- lost temporal windows;
- documents preserving an unfulfilled commitment;
- continued workarounds necessitated by missing repair;
- exclusions maintained by the absence of reconsideration.

```text
archived unresolved item
≠ active residue automatically

remembered absence
≠ present praxis effect automatically
```

The record must show how the residue is carried into present meaning, cost, expectation, access, Binding, Asymmetry, or continuation possibility.

#### Delay and lost alternatives

Delay may be a central form of Residual Accumulation where the expected transition remains absent while the field changes. During delay:

- costs may rise;
- alternatives may expire;
- roles may become specialized;
- temporary workarounds may stabilize;
- expectations may weaken or harden;
- a later occurrence may no longer restore the earlier opportunity.

```text
late occurrence
≠ timely occurrence

later repair
≠ restoration of the earlier possibility automatically
```

Chapter 13 later develops the complete discipline of branches and unavailable alternatives. Chapter 11 records only those alternative effects necessary to establish the bounded Trajectory load.

#### Closure, repair, and non-permanence

Residual Accumulation does not imply permanent unresolvedness. A later event may:

- resolve the expectation;
- narrow its scope;
- compensate a cost;
- reopen an alternative;
- translate the residue into a new Binding;
- leave only a historical trace with no present effect.

```text
resolved Non-Event
≠ no prior residual accumulation

prior residual accumulation
≠ present residue automatically
```

The present analytical cut must distinguish active residue, repaired residue, translated residue, dormant residue, and historical-only trace.

#### Source ceiling and uncertainty

Residual claims are especially vulnerable to retrospective inflation. The analyst must not infer expectation merely because a later outcome would have benefited from an earlier event. Sources may support:

- explicit procedural expectation;
- role-based expectation;
- documented promise or schedule;
- stable repeated practice;
- bounded reconstruction from records;
- contested expectation;
- underdetermined expectation.

Where the expectation or non-occurrence is not sufficiently supported, the result must be reduced. Missing records remain missing information.

#### Profile-specific burden

A bounded Residual Accumulation claim should declare:

```yaml
residual_accumulation:
  path_segment:
  non_event_occurrences:
  expectation_frames:
  expected_windows:
  non_occurrences:
  residual_carriers:
  layering_extension_or_translation:
  alternatives_affected:
  repair_closure_or_dormancy:
  present_effect:
  source_trace:
  uncertainty:
  counterfactual_pressure:
  claim_scope:
```

#### C11-RES-01 — framed Non-Events leaving persistent residue under `Λ + Θ`

A governance process introduces a provisional exception with a documented review date. The review does not occur. Over the next three cycles:

- the exception remains in use;
- participants repeatedly request clarification;
- no final approval or rejection is issued;
- temporary documentation practices become routine;
- one alternative authorization route expires during the delay;
- a later review addresses current use but cannot restore the expired route or recover earlier coordination costs.

The bounded reconstruction is:

```text
warranted review expectation
+ repeated non-review
+ continuing provisional practice
+ expired alternative
+ partial later closure
→ bounded Residual Accumulation
```

The claim distinguishes the documented Non-Events from missing information. It recognizes the later review as repair while preserving the lost temporal window and prior coordination residue. It does not claim that every silence in the process was a Non-Event or that the residue is permanent.

#### Chapter-12 boundary

Residual Accumulation supplies historical load but does not automatically establish that the present could not be reconstructed from current rules and conditions. Chapter 12 separately tests dependence on the actual history.

<a id="11-9-changed-action-corridors"></a>

### 11.9 Changed Action Corridors

A **Changed Action Corridor** is a source-supported difference in the bounded set of continuations that are practically accessible, visible, authorized, affordable, expected, or reversible at the present analytical cut because of the reconstructed historical load.

Changed Action Corridors are not a fifth sedimentation profile, a fourth operation, or a new PMS primitive. They are an integrative present-effect declaration through which the profile-specific historical load becomes praxeologically legible.

```text
Attractor / Asymmetry / Binding / Residual profiles
→ possible corridor effects

corridor effect
≠ new operator
≠ eliminated agency
```

#### Corridor dimensions

An action corridor may be characterized by several distinct dimensions:

- **accessibility:** can the continuation be practically reached?
- **visibility:** is the continuation known or recognizable?
- **authorization:** is it permitted within the declared frame?
- **affordability:** can relevant roles bear its costs?
- **coordination feasibility:** can required actors and resources be aligned?
- **temporal availability:** is the relevant window still open?
- **reversibility:** can the continuation later be revised or exited?
- **expectedness:** is the continuation treated as normal or default?
- **evidentiary burden:** what proof or record is required?
- **exposure:** which roles bear risk or consequence?

The dimensions must not be collapsed. A route can remain formally authorized while becoming practically inaccessible, or become easier to enact while becoming harder to reverse.

```text
formal openness
≠ practical accessibility

practical accessibility
≠ low cost for every role

increased expectedness
≠ mandatory continuation
```

#### Types of corridor change

A Trajectory may produce:

- a **new corridor**, where a continuation becomes available;
- an **easier corridor**, where friction, delay, or coordination burden declines;
- a **harder corridor**, where cost, proof, exposure, or coordination burden rises;
- a **narrowed corridor**, where fewer variants remain practically usable;
- a **closed corridor**, where a formerly available continuation is no longer supported within the claim scope;
- an **institutionalized corridor**, where roles, procedures, or infrastructure stabilize continuation;
- a **conditional corridor**, available only under additional resources or authorization;
- a **reopened corridor**, restored after interruption but not necessarily to its earlier form;
- a **redistributed corridor**, easier for one role and harder for another.

```text
closed within declared frame and period
≠ metaphysically impossible
```

Corridor claims remain frame-, source-, temporal-, and claim-bounded.

#### Historical load and present conditions

A changed corridor must be attributed carefully. The present field may be shaped by:

- Attractor Sedimentation;
- accumulated Asymmetry;
- accumulated Binding;
- Residual Accumulation;
- current rules, resources, actors, technologies, or external events;
- interactions among historical and current conditions.

Chapter 11 requires an allocation statement:

```text
historical contribution:
current-condition contribution:
interaction:
underdetermined allocation:
```

This is not a numerical apportionment requirement. It is a discipline against treating every current constraint as historical merely because a Path can be narrated.

#### Source–Result Dependency

A changed-corridor claim must be counterfactually sensitive:

> Would a relevant change in the alleged historical carrier alter the warranted reconstruction of present continuation possibilities?

Relevant tests may include:

- removing a repeated routine from the Path;
- changing the distribution of prior costs;
- resolving an earlier commitment on time;
- supplying the expected review or repair;
- restoring a lost alternative;
- changing the timing of a threshold event;
- replacing the historical carrier with a current independent rule.

If the corridor conclusion remains unchanged under every relevant source variation, the historical-load claim may exceed the traceability ceiling.

```text
present constraint
+ historical narrative
≠ changed-corridor warrant automatically
```

#### No elimination of agency

A Trajectory can alter continuation possibilities without determining action. A corridor may be narrow, costly, institutionally stabilized, or difficult to exit while still allowing:

- refusal;
- innovation;
- recontextualization;
- Distance;
- repair;
- collective renegotiation;
- external intervention;
- structural break;
- new commitments;
- unanticipated events and Non-Events.

```text
historically altered corridor
≠ determined conduct
≠ prediction
≠ instruction
```

Chapter 11 does not recommend which corridor should be taken.

#### Profile interaction and non-compensation

Changed Action Corridors integrate profile effects without flattening them. A present route may be easier because of an Attractor, harder for one role because of accumulated Asymmetry, expensive to leave because of Binding, and temporally narrowed because of unresolved Non-Events.

The integrated statement must preserve the distinct carriers:

```text
lower coordination friction
+ unequal proof burden
+ high reopening cost
+ expired review window
→ multi-profile corridor change
```

No synthetic strength score is permitted. One profile does not prove another, and a rich multi-profile description does not compensate for unsupported source traces or an overbroad claim.

A Trajectory need not instantiate all four profiles. It must declare:

- which profiles are constitutive;
- which are contextual;
- which are absent;
- which remain uncertain;
- which corridor dimensions each profile changes;
- which present effects are instead produced by current conditions.

#### Repair, reopening, and partial restoration

Repair may reopen a corridor while preserving historical difference. A restored appeal process may require records that were not formerly necessary. A reopened exit may carry sunk costs. A new alternative may be available only to roles with additional resources.

```text
reopened corridor
≠ restored original corridor automatically

partial restoration
≠ no repair
≠ full reset
```

The analyst must record both the repair and the residue.

#### C11-CORR-01 — altered corridor without eliminated agency

A regional network develops a recurring emergency coordination route. Over time:

- the route becomes faster through shared templates and trained staff;
- one central role gains earlier information access;
- participating organizations adapt their systems to the shared format;
- a promised decentralization review is repeatedly delayed;
- a later reform creates a formal alternative route, but using it requires data conversion and independent verification.

At the present cut:

- the central route is easier and more expected;
- the alternative is formally authorized but costlier and less visible;
- exit remains possible;
- current technical standards independently contribute to conversion cost;
- the delayed review explains part, but not all, of the alternative route’s underdevelopment.

The bounded result is:

```text
Attractor Sedimentation:
  lower coordination friction and higher default visibility

Asymmetry Accumulation:
  earlier information access for the central role

Binding Accumulation:
  shared-format reliance and conversion cost

Residual Accumulation:
  delayed decentralization review and underdeveloped alternative

→ changed present corridor:
  central route easier;
  alternative route open but costlier;
  agency retained;
  historical and current contributions separated
```

The result supports a bounded changed-corridor claim. It does not establish inevitability, legal or political legitimacy, person responsibility, strong Path Dependence, or a recommendation to remain in or exit the network.

#### WP2 completion boundary

Sections 11.5–11.9 operationalize the four occurrence-level temporal profiles and their present corridor effects:

```text
C11-ATTR-01
C11-ASYM-01
C11-BIND-01
C11-RES-01
C11-CORR-01
```

WP2 establishes:

- Attractor Sedimentation as changed local friction or default accessibility supported by an `Α` occurrence and temporal carrier;
- Asymmetry Accumulation as retained, redistributed, or hardened differential load supported by an `Ω` occurrence and temporal distribution trace;
- Binding Accumulation as layered or transformed commitment, reliance, breach, or reopening load supported by a `Ψ` occurrence and temporal carrier;
- Residual Accumulation as expectation-bound, source-supported Non-Event residue under `Λ + Θ`;
- Changed Action Corridors as an integrative present-effect declaration rather than a fifth profile or new operation;
- profile separation, non-compensation, source ceiling, current-condition allocation, repair, erosion, dormancy, and retained agency.

The local result remains:

```text
admissible_but_provisional
```

The provisionality identifies no known WP2 defect. It preserves the remaining Chapter-11 burdens:

- Trajectory identity, boundary, and periodization;
- Trajectory Compression and Loss;
- Competing Trajectory Constructions;
- False Trajectory architecture;
- the Minimal Trajectory Record;
- Stop, Non-Capture, integrated audit, and Provisional Lock.

```text
WP2 completion
≠ Chapter 11 provisional lock
≠ all four profiles required in every Trajectory
≠ Path-Dependence confirmation
≠ completed COMPOSE occurrence
≠ RETYPE target-function assignment
≠ operator fusion
≠ authority increase
```

The next controlled production step is:

```text
Chapter 11 — WP3:
Trajectory Boundary,
Trajectory Compression,
Competing Trajectory Constructions,
False Trajectory,
and Minimal Trajectory Record
```

Sections **11.10–11.14**.
<a id="11-10-trajectory-boundary"></a>

### 11.10 Trajectory Boundary

A **Trajectory Boundary** declares where the relevant historical object begins, where the present reconstruction ends, which earlier conditions remain prehistory rather than constitutive segments, and whether continuation beyond the analytical cut remains open.

The boundary is an analytical and claim-sensitive declaration. It is not an ontological discovery of the one true beginning or end of a historical process.

```text
Trajectory Boundary
=
declared temporal and referential scope
of a bounded Trajectory claim

Trajectory Boundary
≠ natural seam in reality
≠ uniquely true periodization
```

A Trajectory cannot be evaluated without a boundary because sedimentation, cumulative change, persistent residue, and Changed Action Corridors are always reconstructed relative to a selected interval and present analytical cut. An earlier start may reveal inherited conditions that a later start treats as given. A later endpoint may show repair, erosion, reversal, or renewed accumulation that an earlier endpoint cannot yet include.

#### Boundary declaration

A minimally responsible boundary declares:

- the **reference object** whose history is being reconstructed;
- the **source frame** and any marked Frame changes;
- the **temporal start** of the claimed Trajectory;
- the **entry rationale** for treating that point or interval as constitutive;
- relevant **prehistory** that conditions the entry but is not included as a full segment;
- the **present analytical cut** or declared terminal interval;
- whether the object is **open, interrupted, dormant, repaired, transformed, or closed** within the claim scope;
- the relation between included segments and any earlier or later Trajectory claims;
- disputed or rival periodizations;
- the claim ceiling generated by the chosen boundary.

```yaml
trajectory_boundary:
  reference_object:
  source_frame:
  temporal_start:
  entry_rationale:
  relevant_prehistory:
  included_segments:
  present_analytical_cut:
  terminal_status:
  open_continuation:
  disputed_periodizations:
  claim_scope:
```

This compact declaration is a prose-level view. It does not create a new Record schema.

#### Beginning the Trajectory

The beginning of a Trajectory should be tied to the earliest **constitutive historical carrier** required for the declared claim, not simply to the oldest available event.

Possible entry rationales include:

- the formation of a recurrent configuration that later changes local friction;
- the first transition that redistributes cost, access, exposure, or exit burden cumulatively;
