Chapter 12 established that a present configuration may depend on its prior Path in weak or strong form, but it did not classify the historical alternatives against which that Path became selective. Chapter 13 now asks a different question:

> Which continuations were genuinely available at an earlier historical cut, and how did realization, rejection, blockage, delay, abortion, or later loss shape the Path that was actually traversed?

A Path is not adequately reconstructed by arranging only what happened. The realized continuation becomes selective only relative to a bounded alternative space: continuations that were materially reachable, conditionally reachable, prepared, contested, rejected, blocked, deferred, begun and interrupted, or later no longer available. This alternative space is not the set of everything that can be imagined after the fact. It is a source-bounded field reconstructed at the earlier cut under the frames, roles, resources, costs, permissions, bindings, knowledge, and temporal windows then in force.

```text
retrospective plausibility
≠ historical availability
```

Chapter 13 therefore protects the realized Path from two opposite distortions:

1. **retrospective linearization** — treating the realized continuation as though no meaningful alternative existed;
2. **counterfactual inflation** — treating any logically imaginable or narratively attractive continuation as though it had been practically available.

Sections 13.1–13.4 establish:

- why PATH must preserve relevant alternatives;
- alternative space as a selective and source-bounded field;
- the minimum burden for a warranted Branch Point;
- the temporal-window and availability test;
- Realized Branch without rationality, legitimacy, optimality, or inevitability inference;
- Rejected Branch as documented non-selection of a materially available continuation;
- the separation of rejection from blockage, impossibility, non-occurrence, and mere absence of evidence;
- five WP1 Pressure Cases.

Sections 13.5–13.8 later distinguish Blocked, Aborted, Deferred, and Lost alternatives. Sections 13.9–13.12 later establish source-bounded Counterfactual Paths, Non-Selection as praxis structure, Alternative-Space Compression, the Alternative Status Record, Failure, Claim Reduction, Stop, and Non-Capture. Chapter 14 retains the full Non-Event analysis. Chapter 15 retains the complete `COMPOSE` procedure. Chapter 46 retains the general Counterfactual-Sensitivity architecture.

<a id="13-1-why-path-must-include-alternatives"></a>

### 13.1 Why PATH Must Include Alternatives

A realized Path is the actually traversed continuation of a reference object through selected configurations and transitions. It is selective because other continuations may have been available, prepared, contested, rejected, blocked, deferred, aborted, or later lost. If those alternatives are removed from the reconstruction, the Path can appear naturally linear, necessary, self-explanatory, or retrospectively inevitable.

```text
realized continuation
≠ necessary continuation
```

PATH must therefore preserve relevant alternatives where they change the warranted reconstruction of:

- what was selectable at the historical cut;
- which costs, permissions, or bindings differentiated the options;
- how an explicit or implicit selection occurred;
- what a non-selection, rejection, blockage, delay, or interruption changed;
- which later continuations remained reachable;
- what residue or loss the unrealized continuation left behind;
- how later `COMPOSE` may compress the former alternative field.

#### Alternative space as a bounded historical field

The **alternative space** is the source-bounded field of continuations that were materially or conditionally reachable at a declared historical cut. It is reconstructed relative to:

- a reference Path or Path candidate;
- a declared configuration and temporal window;
- the actors, roles, institutions, or systems relevant to the claim;
- the frame within which a continuation counted as available;
- current-at-that-time resources, permissions, knowledge, and capacities;
- expected costs, exposures, asymmetries, and binding consequences;
- source support and uncertainty;
- the claim ceiling.

```text
alternative space
≠ all conceivable possibilities
```

An alternative may be:

- **materially available** — the required conditions were present and the continuation could practically be entered;
- **conditionally available** — the continuation was reachable if a declared, source-supported condition was satisfied within the window;
- **prepared** — concrete steps, resources, permissions, or commitments had already been assembled toward it;
- **contested** — actors or structures were actively attempting to preserve or close it;
- **uncertain** — evidence supports candidacy but not a stronger status;
- **unsupported** — later imagination supplies the possibility, but the historical source basis does not.

These local statuses are not canonical Output Classes. They qualify an alternative claim and must map to the fixed output vocabulary according to evidentiary support, boundedness, and claim pressure.

#### Relevance selection inside the alternative space

Chapter 13 does not require exhaustive reconstruction of every minor option, transient thought, or abstract possibility. An alternative is material to the Path claim when its inclusion changes at least one warranted reconstruction concerning:

- selection and non-selection;
- cost or exposure;
- role or authorization;
- timing and window closure;
- binding or reliance;
- reachability of later continuations;
- residue, loss, or irreversibility;
- the validity of a Path-, Trajectory-, or Path-Dependence claim.

An alternative below the Praxeological Relevance Floor adds branching detail without changing the tested Path reconstruction. An alternative above the Traceability Ceiling is a counterfactual label without sufficient historical load.

```text
more branches
≠ more praxeological purchase
```

#### Selection does not require a single selector

A realized continuation may result from:

- an explicit decision;
- a distributed series of local decisions;
- a rule-triggered procedure;
- accumulated commitments;
- a deadline passing;
- repeated non-decision;
- resource allocation;
- asymmetrical access;
- a Non-Event that closes or weakens another continuation.

The presence of selection therefore does not imply one sovereign chooser, a fully articulated intention, or a morally attributable decision. PATH reconstructs the structural production of the realized continuation. It does not infer inner states, guilt, rationality, legitimacy, or responsibility beyond the available claim authority.

#### Alternative preservation and Loss

Alternative-space reconstruction is itself selective. It may preserve:

- material branch points;
- source-supported candidate continuations;
- decisive temporal windows;
- known selection or non-selection traces;
- material blocking or binding structures;
- relevant later reachability;
- residual effects.

It may compress:

- minor variants within one continuation family;
- local tactical options that do not change the Path claim;
- repeated equivalent proposals;
- uncertain micro-windows.

It may exclude:

- purely imagined possibilities;
- unsupported retrospective stories;
- continuations outside the declared frame;
- options that were logically possible but practically unavailable.

It must mark as uncertain or irrecoverable:

- missing deliberation records;
- unclear resource availability;
- inaccessible internal procedures;
- lost evidence about timing;
- alternatives whose former reachability cannot now be reconstructed.

The five-part canonical Loss structure remains the controlling vocabulary. Chapter 13 does not create a sixth alternative-loss field.

#### C13-ALT-01 — selective Path with one source-supported rival

A regional service body had to choose how to maintain access during a twelve-month infrastructure replacement. At the relevant historical cut, records show two funded and technically reviewed continuations:

1. **phased migration** — keep the old interface active while transferring users in three stages;
2. **single-date cutover** — close the old interface and move all users on one declared date.

Both plans had approved budgets, assigned teams, legal clearance, and documented implementation schedules. The body selected the single-date cutover. The phased plan was not implemented, but it remained materially available until the procurement modification deadline.

The realized Path cannot responsibly be described as the only technically possible continuation. Its selectivity is visible because a source-supported rival existed within the same window.

```text
realized cutover
+ materially available phased rival
+ documented common decision window
→ selective Path claim supported
```

The result does not show that the rejected rival would have succeeded, that it was preferable, or that the realized continuation was irrational. It establishes only that the Path was selected from a bounded alternative field. A suitable local mapping is `admissible_with_bounded_claim`.

### 13.2 Branch Point

A **Branch Point** is a declared historical configuration and temporal window at which at least two distinct continuations were source-supported as materially available, conditionally available, prepared, or actively contested.

```text
Branch Point
=
historical cut
+ plural reachable continuations
+ differentiating conditions
+ source support
```

A Branch Point is not every moment at which an analyst can imagine another outcome. It is not created merely because a later history could have unfolded differently. It requires evidence that the alternative field existed at that earlier cut.

```text
narrated fork
≠ warranted Branch Point
```

#### Minimum Branch-Point declaration

A warranted Branch Point must declare:

```yaml
branch_point:
  reference_path:
  source_configuration:
  historical_cut:
  temporal_window:
  governing_frame:
  candidate_continuations:
  availability_basis:
  required_resources_or_permissions:
  expected_costs_and_exposure:
  binding_consequences:
  source_support:
  uncertainty:
  claim_scope:
```

This is a conceptual chapter view, not a second schema. Field completion does not prove the Branch Point.

#### Plurality and distinctness

At least two continuations must be distinct in a way that changes the Path reconstruction. Mere implementation variants within the same continuation do not necessarily establish a Branch Point. Distinctness may concern:

- different target configurations;
- different transition sequences;
- different role or authorization structures;
- different costs or exposure;
- different binding consequences;
- different time windows;
- different later reachability;
- different residue or irreversibility.

```text
multiple descriptions
≠ multiple branches automatically
```

Two proposals that differ only in wording may belong to one continuation family. Conversely, two paths ending in a superficially similar state may remain distinct because they distribute cost, exposure, binding, or residue differently.

#### Historical availability test

Availability must be tested at the historical cut. The analyst asks:

1. Was the continuation recognized, prepared, proposed, procedurally open, or materially reachable then?
2. Were the required resources, permissions, capacities, and role positions present or source-supported as obtainable within the window?
3. Were the expected costs and exposures compatible with practical entry, even if unequal or high?
4. Did a binding, deadline, frame, or authorization rule already close the continuation?
5. Does the source show actual availability, conditional availability, active contestation, or only later imagination?
6. What uncertainty remains?

A continuation is not historically available merely because:

- it became possible later;
- another institution used it elsewhere;
- the technology existed in principle;
- an analyst can describe it coherently;
- a later actor says it “could have been done” without contemporaneous support;
- the realized continuation failed.

```text
possible in principle
≠ available in practice
```

#### Temporal-window discipline

Availability may open, narrow, or close. The relevant window must therefore be declared. A continuation may be available:

- only before a deadline;
- only while a resource remains uncommitted;
- only before a binding is entered;
- only during a regulatory transition;
- only while a role or authorization exists;
- only before a Non-Event accumulates enough delay to change the conditions.

The same continuation label used later may designate a structurally different possibility. Later re-entry does not prove uninterrupted earlier availability.

```text
same later label
≠ same historical branch
```

#### Costly availability

High cost does not automatically make a continuation unavailable. Availability may be asymmetrical, burdensome, risky, or politically difficult while remaining materially reachable. The claim must distinguish:

- **unavailable** — required conditions were absent or the continuation was closed;
- **available at high cost** — entry remained possible but carried a declared burden;
- **conditionally available** — entry depended on a realizable condition;
- **blocked** — an identifiable structure prevented entry despite prior material availability;
- **uncertain** — the source cannot responsibly classify the status.

This distinction prevents an expensive alternative from being rewritten as impossible and prevents a merely logical option from being treated as practically available.

#### Later knowledge and retrospective leakage

Later outcomes may help identify which sources to inspect, but they may not be imported into the earlier availability judgment. The analyst must not infer that:

- the realized branch was available because it later succeeded;
- the unrealized branch was unavailable because it was not chosen;
- a later failure proves that an earlier rival was viable;
- a later technology or permission existed at the earlier cut;
- a later narrative establishes what actors could then know.

```text
later outcome
≠ earlier availability evidence automatically
```

#### C13-BRANCH-01 — bounded Branch Point with three available continuations

A research consortium faced a six-week decision window after a funding reduction. Contemporaneous records identify three continuations:

- reduce the project scope while keeping all partners;
- retain the scope but postpone one work package for a year;
- replace one partner with a lower-cost provider.

Each continuation had:

- a written implementation outline;
- a budget estimate;
- identified approval requirements;
- at least one authorized sponsor;
- a deadline before the next funding release.

The options differed materially in role allocation, expected delay, binding consequences, and later reachability. The consortium selected scope reduction.

The Branch Point is warranted because the plural continuations and common temporal window are source-supported. The analysis does not infer which alternative would have produced the best outcome.

```text
plural prepared continuations
+ common historical window
+ differentiating cost, role, and binding consequences
→ warranted Branch Point
```

A bounded local mapping is `admissible_with_bounded_claim`.

#### C13-SOURCE-01 — realized continuation known, alternative field unsupported

A public archive confirms that a municipal office adopted a centralized intake system in 1998. Later commentary claims that a decentralized district model was “the obvious alternative.” The available historical sources show:

- the centralized decision and implementation;
- no district-model proposal;
- no budget, permission, staffing, or technical plan for decentralization;
- no evidence that district offices had the required authority;
- later decentralized systems in other cities, introduced under different law and technology.

The realized branch is known. The alternative space is not.

```text
realized continuation supported
+ rival only retrospectively plausible
+ no historical availability trace
→ no warranted Branch Point claim
```

The Path may still be reconstructed as realized. The alternative claim must be reduced, withheld, or classified as unsupported. A strong Branch-Point assertion would require `claim_reduction_required` or `failed_transformation` depending on how centrally it supports the broader claim. Lack of alternative-space evidence is not proof that no alternative existed; it marks the source ceiling.

### 13.3 Realized Branch

A **Realized Branch** is the continuation actually traversed from a warranted Branch Point or bounded alternative field.

```text
Realized Branch
=
actually entered continuation
+ source-traceable traversal
```

The Realized Branch remains part of the Path. “Branch” adds the claim that the continuation was selected relative to at least one relevant alternative. Where the alternative field is unsupported, the analyst may retain the realized continuation without calling it a branch in the stronger sense.

#### Realization and traversal

Realization requires more than formal announcement or stated preference. The continuation must have been entered through source-supported transition traces such as:

- authorization or commitment;
- allocation of resources;
- role reassignment;
- initiation of implementation;
- execution of an event sequence;
- closure of incompatible continuations;
- persistence into the next configuration.

A proposal may be selected but never realized. An announced continuation may be blocked before entry. A plan may begin and later become an Aborted Branch. Chapter 13 therefore distinguishes decision, entry, continuation, interruption, and later completion.

```text
selection statement
≠ realized traversal automatically
```

#### Explicit, implicit, and distributed realization

A Realized Branch may arise through:

- **explicit selection** — a documented decision chooses one continuation;
- **implicit selection** — conduct repeatedly follows one route while alternatives remain unentered;
- **distributed selection** — multiple local decisions cumulatively produce one continuation;
- **procedural selection** — a rule or threshold activates one route;
- **Non-Event selection** — failure to decide, respond, renew, or act allows a window to close and leaves one continuation as the traversed Path.

Chapter 14 later owns the full Non-Event test. WP1 only preserves that a realized route may emerge without one positive decision event.

#### No rationality, legitimacy, or inevitability inference

Realization does not establish that the continuation was:

- rational;
- optimal;
- legitimate;
- morally justified;
- intended by every relevant actor;
- causally necessary;
- predictable;
- the only available route.

```text
realized
≠ rational
≠ legitimate
≠ inevitable
```

Likewise, continued persistence does not prove initial consent, endorsement, or appropriateness. PATH reconstructs traversal and selection structure, not a tribunal judgment.

#### Selection trace and uncertainty

The analyst should distinguish:

- what was explicitly selected;
- what was entered without explicit selection;
- what became realized through accumulated local actions;
- what resulted from a closing window or Non-Event;
- what remains uncertain about who or what carried the transition.

A Realized Branch claim may be strong even where the selector is distributed, provided traversal and the alternative field are source-traceable. Conversely, a clear decision record does not prove realization if implementation never begins.

#### Residual relation to unrealized branches

The Realized Branch may retain relations to unrealized continuations through:

- sunk preparation cost;
- preserved records or dormant plans;
- disappointed expectations;
- binding consequences;
- later reopening pressure;
- contested legitimacy claims;
- changed later reachability.

These residual effects do not make the unrealized continuation realized. They show that non-realization can remain structurally active.

#### C13-REAL-01 — realization through implicit and Non-Event selection

A university department had two source-supported scheduling continuations for a new program:

1. reserve a dedicated weekly block before the annual timetable freeze;
2. distribute sessions across existing departmental slots.

The dedicated block required a formal reservation by 30 June. Meeting minutes show repeated discussion but no reservation request. On 1 July the central scheduler closed the window. The department then distributed sessions across existing slots and continued that arrangement for the full academic year.

There was no single recorded vote choosing distributed scheduling. The realized continuation emerged through:

- repeated non-submission before the deadline;
- closure of the reservation window;
- subsequent allocation of sessions across existing slots;
- actual traversal of the distributed schedule.

```text
available dedicated-block branch
+ decision context and deadline
+ non-submission closes window
+ distributed route actually traversed
→ Realized Branch through implicit/Non-Event selection
```

The analysis does not infer that the route was rational, intended by all members, or inevitable. The positive Path claim can be `admissible_with_bounded_claim`, while the full `Λ` characterization remains for Chapter 14.

### 13.4 Rejected Branch

A **Rejected Branch** is a materially or conditionally available continuation that was explicitly or source-traceably declined, refused, voted down, withdrawn from selection, or otherwise documented as not selected while its relevant availability window remained open.

```text
Rejected Branch
=
available continuation
+ documented non-selection or refusal
+ open relevant window
```

```text
not realized
≠ rejected automatically
```

A continuation may remain unrealized because it was blocked, never prepared, deferred, abandoned before becoming available, rendered unavailable by a closing window, or simply unsupported by the sources. Rejection is a specific status with its own evidentiary burden.

#### Rejection trace

A Rejected Branch may be supported by:

- a vote against the continuation;
- a documented refusal;
- a decision memorandum selecting another route and excluding this one;
- withdrawal after evaluation while the option remained open;
- a recorded instruction not to proceed;
- repeated, attributable non-selection within a declared active decision procedure.

Silence alone does not establish rejection unless the frame and decision context make non-response a source-supported refusal. Chapter 14 later owns the deeper `Λ` analysis.

#### Rejected versus blocked

The distinction is structural:

- **rejected:** the continuation remained materially available, but it was not selected;
- **blocked:** an identifiable structure prevented entry despite material availability or preparation.

A party may reject an option because it is costly. A party may also claim rejection when authorization was already denied. The source must show whether non-selection occurred inside an open option field or whether the route was structurally prevented.

```text
refusal under open availability
≠ prevention under closed access
```

Where the sources cannot distinguish rejection from blockage, the status must remain provisional, reduced, or non-captured. Chapter 13 must not choose the more dramatic label.

#### Rejection and later reachability

Rejection at one cut does not establish permanent loss. The branch may later:

- remain available;
- reopen under the same frame;
- reopen only under changed conditions;
- become deferred;
- become structurally different;
- become unavailable or lost;
- leave residue without later reachability.

The Rejected Branch claim therefore records the historical window and later status separately.

```text
rejected then
≠ unavailable forever
```

#### Residual effects

Rejection may leave:

- sunk design or preparation costs;
- disappointed reliance;
- recorded dissent;
- altered trust or credibility;
- changed role relations;
- a preserved proposal that later re-enters;
- later recontextualization pressure;
- a narrowed or widened subsequent alternative field.

These effects must be source-supported and claim-bounded. Rejection does not automatically generate a Residual-Dependence claim, a moral finding, or a target function.

#### Rejection and claim ceiling

A Rejected Branch claim may establish:

- that an available continuation was not selected;
- who or what recorded the non-selection where supported;
- the relevant decision frame and temporal window;
- immediate residual effects;
- later reachability status where evidenced.

It may not establish without separate authority:

- that rejection was wrong or irrational;
- that the rejected route would have worked;
- that the realized route caused later harm;
- that a person or institution has a fixed type;
- that the rejected branch should now be restored;
- that rejection authorizes intervention or sanction.

#### C13-REJ-01 — explicit rejection with preserved residue

A hospital network reviewed two materially available procurement routes for a records platform:

- extend the existing vendor contract for three years;
- migrate to a jointly operated public platform.

The public-platform route had legal approval, a completed cost model, an implementation partner, and a valid offer until 31 March. On 10 March, the governing board voted to reject that route and approved the vendor extension.

The rejected route left source-supported residue:

- six months of design work remained archived;
- two partner institutions had already assigned staff;
- the rejection created documented withdrawal costs;
- the public offer expired on 31 March;
- a later public-platform proposal required a new legal and technical design and was not simply the same uninterrupted branch.

```text
materially available continuation
+ explicit refusal while window open
+ source-supported residual effects
→ Rejected Branch
```

The finding does not prove that migration would have succeeded or that the rejection was illegitimate. A bounded result may map to `admissible_with_bounded_claim`.

#### WP1 completion boundary

Sections 13.1–13.4 now canonically establish:

- alternative space as a source-bounded field of historically reachable continuations rather than all imaginable possibilities;
- realized Path selectivity without retrospective linearization;
- materiality and Relevance-Floor control for alternative inclusion;
- preservation, compression, exclusion, uncertainty, and irrecoverability under the existing five-part Loss structure;
- Branch Point as a historical cut with plural, distinct, source-supported continuations and a declared temporal window;
- practical availability distinguished from logical possibility, later possibility, high-cost availability, conditional availability, and uncertainty;
- protection against retrospective leakage from later outcomes;
- Realized Branch as actually traversed continuation, including explicit, implicit, distributed, procedural, and bounded Non-Event selection;
- realization without rationality, legitimacy, optimality, inevitability, intention, or prediction inference;
- Rejected Branch as documented non-selection or refusal while availability remained open;
- strict separation of rejection from blockage, impossibility, mere non-realization, and unsupported alternatives;
- later reachability and residual effects as separate, source-bounded claims;
- all five assigned WP1 Pressure Cases.

The local result is:

```text
admissible_but_provisional
```

The provisionality identifies no known WP1 repair requirement. It preserves the remaining Chapter-13 duties:

- WP2 classification of Blocked, Aborted, Deferred, and Lost alternatives;
- WP3 Counterfactual Path, Non-Selection, Alternative-Space Compression, Alternative Status Record, Failure, Reduction, Stop, Non-Capture, and schema decision;
- WP4 integrated audit and Provisional Lock;
- later Chapter-14 Non-Event detail;
- later Chapter-15 `COMPOSE` procedure;
- later Chapter-46 general Counterfactual-Sensitivity architecture.

```text
WP1 completion
≠ Chapter 13 provisional lock
≠ full branch taxonomy
≠ lost-alternative proof
≠ counterfactual truth
≠ completed COMPOSE occurrence
≠ target-function assignment
≠ authority increase
```

The next controlled production step is:

```text
Chapter 13 — WP2:
Blocked Branch,
Aborted Branch,
Deferred Branch,
and Lost Alternative
```
<a id="13-5-blocked-branch"></a>

### 13.5 Blocked Branch

A **Blocked Branch** is a continuation that was materially or conditionally available, prepared, or actively being entered, but was prevented from proceeding within the relevant historical window by an identifiable blocking structure.

```text
available or prepared continuation
+ identifiable prevention relation
+ bounded historical window
→ Blocked Branch candidate
```

The blocking structure may consist of:

- a frame that excludes the continuation from authorized consideration;
- a missing permission or procedural approval;
- a resource constraint that becomes operative inside the window;
- an asymmetry of access, exposure, or decision capacity;
- an existing binding that prevents entry without a prior release;
- a deadline, dependency, or threshold that cannot be satisfied in time;
- a repeated Non-Event that leaves a required transition unrealized;
- a combination of these relations.

Chapter 13 does not treat a branch as blocked merely because it was not realized. Non-realization may also reflect rejection, deferral, loss, lack of support, or the absence of a real alternative. A Blocked-Branch claim requires both earlier availability and a source-supported prevention relation.

```text
not realized
≠ blocked automatically
```

#### Blocked is not impossible

A blocked continuation is not necessarily impossible in principle, impossible under every frame, or permanently unavailable. The claim is local:

```text
blocked in window W under frame F
≠ impossible across all later windows and frames
```

A continuation can be:

- blocked only for one actor or role;
- blocked at one procedural stage but open at another;
- blocked temporarily and later reopened;
- blocked under one binding structure but available after release;
- blocked at tolerable cost for one party but inaccessible at that cost for another;
- blocked in practice despite formal availability;
- formally unavailable while informally attempted.

The analyst must therefore declare the **scope**, **duration**, and **relational location** of the blockage. A broad statement such as “the option was impossible” is inadmissible where the sources support only a bounded prevention claim.

#### Blocking structure and source burden

A warranted Blocked-Branch claim should identify:

```yaml
blocked_branch:
  reference_path:
  branch_point:
  historical_window:
  candidate_continuation:
  prior_availability_basis:
  blocking_structure:
  blocking_relation:
  affected_role_or_access_path:
  prevention_window:
  later_reachability:
  residual_effect:
  source_support:
  uncertainty:
  claim_scope:
```

This is a chapter-level declaration view, not a new schema or Output Class. Field presence does not prove that the continuation was blocked.

Sources should support at least:

1. that the continuation was practically available, conditionally available, prepared, or actively attempted before the blockage;
2. that a specific frame, resource, permission, asymmetry, binding, dependency, deadline, or Non-Event prevented entry or continuation within the claimed window;
3. that the prevention relation mattered to the realized Path;
4. the degree to which the continuation remained reachable later.

A simple statement by a later participant that “we were prevented” may be relevant evidence but is not automatically sufficient. The source basis should distinguish retrospective explanation from contemporaneous blocking traces where possible.

#### Rejection and blockage may coexist at different cuts

Rejected and Blocked are distinct statuses, but the same continuation can receive different statuses at different historical cuts.

For example:

```text
window 1:
continuation available and explicitly rejected

window 2:
same-labelled continuation newly proposed but blocked by a later rule
```

Or:

```text
entry attempted
→ permission withheld
→ continuation blocked
→ actors later abandon it
```

The later abandonment does not retroactively convert the earlier blockage into rejection. Status claims must remain cut-specific.

#### Blocking does not allocate blame or authority

A blocking structure may be distributed across rules, roles, timing, resources, and bindings. Chapter 13 reconstructs prevention relations. It does not infer:

- guilt;
- illegitimacy;
- intent to obstruct;
- moral responsibility;
- legal invalidity;
- entitlement to override the block;
- an intervention recommendation.

```text
blocking structure
≠ person verdict
≠ authorization to remove the block
```

#### C13-BLOCK-01 — available continuation prevented by a temporary authorization barrier

A municipal health network prepared a shared mobile clinic route for three rural districts. Before the winter service window:

- vehicles and staff were assigned;
- the route plan had passed technical review;
- all three districts had signed participation letters;
- funding was available for the six-month pilot.

The pilot required a temporary cross-district operating authorization. The authorization board postponed its decision beyond the statutory start date because one required member position remained vacant. Without the authorization, the vehicles could not legally operate across district boundaries during that winter window.

The sources support:

```text
prepared and funded continuation
+ required authorization
+ decision postponed beyond operative window
+ no lawful cross-district entry during that window
→ Blocked Branch
```

The continuation was not impossible in principle. A new board approved a revised pilot the following year. The later pilot used different staffing, costs, and routing and therefore does not erase the earlier bounded blockage or establish uninterrupted continuity.

The claim does not establish that any person intentionally obstructed the pilot or that the later approval repaired every loss. A suitable local result is `admissible_with_bounded_claim`.

#### C13-CONF-01 — rejection or blockage cannot be responsibly distinguished

A university archive contains a proposal for a joint digitization program, a later note stating that the program “did not proceed,” and a budget sheet showing that the required matching funds were not released. No minutes survive from the decision meeting. Later interviews conflict:

- one participant recalls an explicit vote against the program;
- another recalls that the program was supported but could not proceed without the funds;
- neither account is independently corroborated.

The sources establish that the program was considered and that it was not realized. They do not responsibly distinguish:

```text
explicit rejection
from
resource blockage
```

The branch may be recorded as a source-supported unrealized candidate with unresolved status. A claim that it was definitively rejected or definitively blocked must be reduced. Where the distinction remains material and undecidable, the local result may be `non_capture`.

### 13.6 Aborted Branch

An **Aborted Branch** is a continuation that was actually initiated and partially traversed, but was interrupted before the claimed transition, phase, or continuation had been completed.

```text
initiation trace
+ partial traversal
+ interruption before claimed continuation completion
→ Aborted Branch candidate
```

An Aborted Branch is not a merely proposed continuation, a rejected proposal, or a branch that never became available. It requires an **initiation trace**.

#### Initiation burden

Initiation may be supported by:

- committed resources;
- activated procedures;
- signed implementation orders;
- actual movement of participants, data, goods, or responsibilities;
- completed first-stage transitions;
- incurred entry costs;
- operational use of partial infrastructure;
- binding commitments that only make sense after entry into the continuation.

```text
announcement
≠ initiation

planning
≠ partial traversal automatically
```

The analyst must identify the point at which preparation became entry. That threshold depends on the reference object and claim. A signed memorandum may initiate one institutional Path while remaining mere preparation for another operational Path.

#### Interruption burden

The interruption must be located relative to the claimed continuation. It may result from:

- a new block;
- withdrawal of a necessary participant;
- loss of resources;
- failure of an expected transition;
- explicit termination;
- changed frame or authorization;
- a threshold not reached after partial entry;
- an external event that ends the operational window.

The interruption trace should show what stopped, when it stopped, and what remained after the stop. Chapter 13 need not prove a complete causal history. It must establish enough source-linked relation to support the bounded status claim.

#### Aborted is not never begun

```text
begun and interrupted
≠ never begun
```

Treating an aborted continuation as though it never existed can erase:

- sunk costs;
- created expectations;
- partial infrastructure;
- changed roles;
- temporary dependencies;
- disclosed information;
- accumulated commitments;
- new constraints on later alternatives.

An aborted branch may materially shape the later Path even if its intended endpoint was never reached.

#### Aborted can coexist with blockage, rejection, or later resumption

The status describes traversal, not necessarily the interruption mechanism.

A branch may be:

```text
initiated
→ later blocked
→ aborted because traversal cannot continue
```

Or:

```text
initiated
→ later explicitly terminated
→ aborted through rejection of further continuation
```

The analyst may therefore record a primary traversal status and a separate interruption relation. No universal single-status rule is required.

A later continuation with the same label is not automatically the same branch resumed. Continuity requires comparison of:

- reference object;
- frame;
- participants and roles;
- resources and permissions;
- temporal gap;
- preserved infrastructure;
- binding continuity;
- transition lineage;
- unresolved residue.

```text
same label after interruption
≠ uninterrupted branch
≠ resumed branch automatically
```

#### C13-ABORT-01 — initiated migration interrupted after partial traversal

A public library consortium began migrating its catalogue to a shared platform. The sources show:

- contracts were signed;
- forty percent of records were transferred;
- staff began using the shared authentication system;
- two local systems were decommissioned;
- migration support costs were incurred.

During the second phase, the platform provider lost the required security certification. The remaining transfers stopped, the consortium terminated the migration order, and the libraries restored separate temporary systems.

The branch had been entered and partially traversed. It was not merely planned.

```text
actual data transfer
+ decommissioned local systems
+ active shared authentication
+ interruption before full migration
→ Aborted Branch
```

The aborted branch left residue:

- restoration costs;
- mixed record formats;
- staff retraining;
- unresolved ownership questions for transferred records;
- reduced availability of the previous local systems.

A later consortium platform adopted three years afterward used a different provider and architecture. It cannot be treated as the same uninterrupted continuation without a separate continuity claim. A bounded local mapping is `admissible_with_bounded_claim`.

### 13.7 Deferred Branch

A **Deferred Branch** is a continuation whose entry or further traversal is shifted beyond its initially relevant temporal window while remaining, at least provisionally, a candidate for later continuation.

```text
source-supported continuation
+ displacement beyond initial window
+ later candidacy or reachability
→ Deferred Branch candidate
```

Deferral is not temporal emptiness. The delay is itself part of the realized Path because conditions can change while the continuation is postponed.

```text
deferred continuation
≠ unchanged continuation waiting outside time
```

#### Deferral burden

A warranted Deferred-Branch claim should declare:

```yaml
deferred_branch:
  reference_path:
  original_branch_point:
  original_window:
  candidate_continuation:
  original_availability_basis:
  deferral_trace:
  new_or_open_window:
  intervening_changes:
  continuity_basis:
  later_reachability:
  residual_effect:
  source_support:
  uncertainty:
  claim_scope:
```

A deferral trace may consist of:

- an explicit postponement decision;
- a revised timetable;
- a condition that must be satisfied before reconsideration;
- repeated non-decision inside an active decision context;
- procedural suspension;
- temporary preservation of resources or permissions for later use;
- a documented decision to keep the continuation open without entering it.

Chapter 14 retains the full burden for determining when repeated non-decision constitutes a warranted `Λ` occurrence. Chapter 13 only records the temporal and branch consequences needed for the status claim.

#### Delay changes the branch

During deferral, any of the following may change:

- the governing frame;
- available resources;
- costs and exposure;
- participants and roles;
- legal or technical permissions;
- dependencies and bindings;
- expected benefits or burdens;
- competing continuations;
- the practical meaning of entry;
- the availability window itself.

A later continuation may therefore be:

- a preserved continuation with bounded changes;
- a resumed continuation after interruption;
- a newly constituted continuation using some inherited material;
- a same-labelled but structurally different proposal;
- no longer available;
- unresolved under the available sources.

```text
later continuation with same name
≠ original branch preserved automatically
```

#### Deferred is not uninterrupted

An uninterrupted continuation preserves traversal across the relevant temporal relation. A deferred continuation includes a declared gap in entry or progression.

That gap may matter even where the endpoint later resembles the originally proposed endpoint. It can alter:

- who bears the cost;
- which infrastructure is used;
- which alternatives close;
- what dependencies accumulate;
- which commitments are still valid;
- what the later endpoint means.

```text
similar later endpoint
≠ uninterrupted Path
```

#### Deferred is not rejected, blocked, or lost automatically

- **Rejected:** the continuation is declined or not selected while available.
- **Blocked:** a prevention relation stops entry or progression within the window.
- **Deferred:** entry or progression is shifted while later candidacy remains.
- **Lost:** later availability or material reachability no longer remains.

These statuses can change over time. A deferred branch can later become realized, rejected, blocked, aborted, lost, or uncertain. The analyst must not freeze the initial status across all later cuts.

#### C13-DEFER-01 — postponement changes the later continuation

A coastal authority approved a flood-barrier project in principle but postponed construction for five years while awaiting a national funding program. At the original decision cut:

- the route was technically approved;
- land access agreements were valid;
- local co-financing was reserved;
- the project could have begun within eighteen months.

The authority adopted a formal postponement resolution and retained the project in its capital plan.

During the five-year deferral:

- two land agreements expired;
- construction prices increased substantially;
- a protected habitat designation altered the permitted route;
- one municipality withdrew its co-financing commitment;
- emergency pumping infrastructure was installed as an interim measure.

The project was later restarted under a new route, new cost allocation, and new legal approval.

```text
originally available project
+ explicit postponement
+ material intervening change
+ later structurally revised continuation
→ Deferred Branch with changed lineage
```

The later project may retain historical continuity with the earlier proposal, but it is not the same uninterrupted continuation. A bounded result may be `admissible_with_bounded_claim`.

#### C13-CONF-02 — deferred versus uninterrupted continuation

A research consortium describes a ten-year program as one continuous initiative. The archive, however, shows:

- an initial three-year funded phase;
- a two-year period with no staff, budget, data collection, or valid consortium agreement;
- a later grant using the same program name but a revised research question, new partners, and a different data standard.

The shared name and retrospective narrative support some lineage claim. They do not support uninterrupted traversal.

```text
same label
+ multi-year inactive gap
+ new funding, partners, and object
≠ uninterrupted continuation
```

The sources may support either:

- an early branch followed by deferral and later reconstitution;
- an aborted first branch and a new later branch;
- unresolved continuity.

Where the evidence cannot discriminate among these options, the status must remain bounded or receive `non_capture`. The continuity shortcut is inadmissible.

### 13.8 Lost Alternative

A **Lost Alternative** is a continuation that was earlier source-supported as materially available, conditionally available, prepared, or actively contested, but later became unavailable or materially unreachable within the declared frame and claim scope.

```text
earlier availability
+ later unavailability or material unreachability
+ source-supported loss transition
→ Lost Alternative candidate
```

A Lost Alternative is not a free counterfactual possibility. It requires evidence of former reachability.

```text
later desired option
≠ formerly available option
```

#### Earlier availability is mandatory

The analyst must first establish the earlier availability claim under the burdens of Sections 13.1–13.4. If the sources cannot show that the continuation was materially or conditionally reachable at the earlier cut, later unavailability does not create a Lost Alternative.

A statement such as “we can no longer do X” supports current unavailability. It does not by itself prove that X was historically available.

#### Loss mechanisms

A continuation may become unavailable or materially unreachable through source-supported changes involving:

- `Θ`: expiry of a temporal window, irreversible sequencing, or elapsed coordination conditions;
- `Ω`: accumulated cost, exposure, role asymmetry, or access loss;
- `Ψ`: commitments, contracts, reliance, or self-binding that close incompatible continuations;
- `Λ`: missed review, absent repair, non-decision, or failure to act within a bounded window;
- `□`: changed legal, institutional, technical, or relational frame;
- resource depletion or infrastructure removal;
- capability or permission loss;
- an irreversible transition in the realized Path.

The presence of an operator occurrence does not classify the branch automatically.

```text
Θ, Ω, Ψ, Λ, or □ occurrence
≠ Lost Alternative automatically
```

The claim must show how the relevant relation altered later reachability.

#### Lost is not merely unattractive or expensive

A continuation is not lost merely because:

- it became less desirable;
- expected returns fell;
- political support weakened;
- the cost increased;
- another option became preferable;
- participants no longer wished to pursue it.

High cost can constitute material unreachability where the cost exceeds source-supported available capacity, authorization, or practical possibility inside the claim frame. But cost increase alone does not establish loss.

```text
currently unattractive
≠ unavailable

more expensive
≠ materially unreachable automatically
```

#### Lost is not temporarily blocked

A temporarily blocked branch may remain reachable after a permission, dependency, or resource condition changes. A Lost Alternative claim requires stronger support that the earlier continuation no longer remains available within the declared frame or that restoration would constitute a new continuation rather than reopening the old one.

The loss may be:

- local to one actor or institution;
- local to one temporal window;
- local to one frame or resource configuration;
- durable but not metaphysically irreversible;
- partial, where some continuation family survives but the earlier form does not;
- uncertain where later reachability cannot be reconstructed.

#### Later equivalent does not restore the same alternative automatically

A later technology, policy, institution, or actor may enable a functionally similar continuation. That does not prove that the earlier alternative remained continuously available or that its loss was reversed.

```text
later functional equivalent
≠ preserved historical alternative
```

The later continuation may require:

- a new frame;
- new resources;
- different permissions;
- different actors;
- a new binding structure;
- new costs and exposures;
- a separate Branch Point.

Such a continuation must receive its own testable claim.

#### Lost Alternative and canonical Loss are distinct

`Lost Alternative` is a branch-status claim about a historical continuation. The canonical five-part `loss` declaration records what a STRATA representation or transformation preserves, compresses, excludes, leaves uncertain, or renders irrecoverable.

```text
Lost Alternative
≠ sixth canonical Loss field
```

A record concerning a Lost Alternative may still declare:

```yaml
loss:
  preserved:
  compressed:
  excluded:
  uncertain:
  irrecoverable:
```

The status claim and the representation-loss declaration must not be collapsed.

#### C13-LOST-01 — earlier available route later closed by irreversible commitment and window expiry

A regional rail authority considered two depot locations before ordering a new fleet. At the historical decision cut:

- both sites had completed environmental review;
- both had valid purchase options;
- either could support the planned fleet;
- funding covered one depot;
- both options expired at the end of the fiscal year.

The authority selected Site A and signed a twenty-five-year access contract. It allowed the purchase option for Site B to expire. Two years later, the land at Site B was incorporated into a protected water reserve, and rail construction there was prohibited under the new plan.

The sources support:

```text
earlier materially available Site-B continuation
+ unexercised time-limited option
+ expiry
+ later incompatible land-use frame
→ Lost Alternative within the original depot Path
```

The claim does not prove that Site B would have produced better outcomes or that the selection of Site A was wrongful. A later proposal for a depot near Site B would require new land, new approval, and a new Branch Point. It would not automatically restore the earlier alternative. A bounded local mapping is `admissible_with_bounded_claim`.

#### WP2 status matrix

| Status | Minimum positive burden | Must remain distinct from |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Blocked Branch | earlier availability or preparation plus an identifiable prevention relation inside the window | rejected, impossible, merely unrealized |
| Aborted Branch | initiation and partial traversal plus interruption before claimed completion | never begun, merely planned, rejected before entry |
| Deferred Branch | source-supported continuation shifted beyond its initial window with later candidacy or reachability | uninterrupted, rejected, blocked, lost |
| Lost Alternative | earlier availability plus later unavailability or material unreachability and a source-supported loss transition | unattractive, expensive, temporarily blocked, imagined |

These are local branch-status findings, not canonical Output Classes. They may coexist across different cuts or describe different aspects of one continuation. Each must map to the fixed output vocabulary according to support, boundedness, uncertainty, and claim pressure.

#### WP2 completion boundary

Sections 13.5–13.8 now canonically establish:

- Blocked Branch as a previously available, prepared, or attempted continuation prevented within a declared window by an identifiable blocking structure;
- blockage as distinct from rejection, impossibility, mere non-realization, and unsupported retrospective explanation;
- scope, duration, relational location, later reachability, and residual effect as required bounded declarations;
- Aborted Branch as actual initiation and partial traversal followed by interruption before the claimed continuation is complete;
- initiation trace as mandatory and announcement or planning as insufficient;
- preservation of sunk costs, expectations, infrastructure, bindings, and residues after abortion;
- status/cause separation where an initiated branch is later blocked, rejected, or otherwise interrupted;
- Deferred Branch as temporal displacement beyond the original window while later candidacy remains;
- delay as a Path-forming interval that may change frame, resource, cost, role, binding, and later identity;
- later same-labelled continuation as requiring an independent continuity claim;
- Lost Alternative as earlier source-supported availability followed by later unavailability or material unreachability;
- lost as distinct from unattractive, expensive, temporarily blocked, or retrospectively imagined;
- `Θ`, `Ω`, `Ψ`, `Λ`, and `□` as possible loss-bearing occurrences without automatic status classification;
- later functional equivalence as insufficient to restore the same historical alternative;
- Lost Alternative as distinct from the canonical five-part `loss` declaration;
- all six assigned WP2 Pressure Cases.

The local result is:

```text
admissible_but_provisional
```

The provisionality identifies no known WP2 repair requirement. It preserves the remaining Chapter-13 duties:

- WP3 Counterfactual Path, Non-Selection, Alternative-Space Compression, Alternative Status Record, Failure, Reduction, Stop, Non-Capture, and schema decision;
- WP4 integrated audit and Provisional Lock;
- later Chapter-14 Non-Event detail;
- later Chapter-15 `COMPOSE` procedure;
- later Chapter-46 general Counterfactual-Sensitivity architecture;
- later Reader graph implementation without status inference.

```text
WP2 completion
≠ Chapter 13 provisional lock
≠ completed counterfactual Path
≠ completed COMPOSE occurrence
≠ branch-status truth engine
≠ causal or normative proof
≠ target-function assignment
≠ authority increase
```

The next controlled production step is:

```text
Chapter 13 — WP3:
Counterfactual Path,
Non-Selection,
Alternative-Space Compression,
and Alternative Status Record
```

<a id="13-9-counterfactual-path"></a>

### 13.9 Counterfactual Path

A **Counterfactual Path** is a bounded hypothetical continuation that begins from a source-supported historical alternative at a declared Branch Point and explores only those subsequent transitions for which the available sources, constraints, and declared variation rule continue to carry reconstructive load.

```text
historically available continuation
+ declared variation at the Branch Point
+ source-bounded continuation pressure
→ Counterfactual Path candidate
```

A Counterfactual Path is not an unrestricted story about what might have happened. It does not convert historical availability into knowledge of unrealized outcomes. The sources may establish that a continuation could have been entered while remaining insufficient to establish how that continuation would have developed.

```text
available branch
≠ known unrealized future
```

#### Counterfactual entry burden

A Counterfactual Path may begin only where the Chapter-13 availability burden has already been met. It must declare:

- the reference Path and historical Branch Point;
- the candidate continuation and its availability status at that cut;
- the temporal window within which entry remained possible;
- the source basis for availability;
- the realized continuation from which the hypothetical continuation diverges;
- the bounded variation introduced at the divergence;
- the conditions held approximately stable for the comparison;
- the first point at which sources no longer support further reconstruction;
- uncertainty, Loss, claim scope, and Stop condition.

An analyst may not manufacture a Counterfactual Path by starting from a continuation that was merely logically possible, technically imaginable in a later period, or narratively attractive after the realized outcome became known.

#### One bounded divergence, not total historical rewriting

The variation rule should be as small and explicit as the question permits. It may alter:

- one selection at a Branch Point;
- one documented permission or resource condition;
- one bounded Non-Selection;
- one source-supported timing relation;
- one earlier available continuation.

It must not silently rewrite all later actors, costs, institutions, knowledge, and constraints in order to preserve the preferred counterfactual story. Once the altered continuation would itself change later conditions, those changes must be declared rather than treated as though the realized world's later structure remained automatically available.

```text
bounded divergence
≠ wholesale historical substitution
```

#### Source ceiling and counterfactual horizon

The **counterfactual horizon** is the last point at which the hypothetical continuation remains reconstructively anchored. Beyond that point, the record may identify open possibilities or unresolved consequences, but it may not continue as a warranted Path.

The horizon may be reached because:

- later transitions were never planned or documented;
- actor responses are unknown;
- resource or authorization consequences become indeterminate;
- multiple equally supported continuations emerge;
- the altered branch changes the frame too extensively;
- later evidence depends on the realized Path and cannot be transferred;
- the claim would require causal or predictive authority not supplied by STRATA.

A responsible Counterfactual Path may therefore be very short. A one-transition counterfactual that stops at the source ceiling is methodologically stronger than a complete alternative history produced by unsupported continuity assumptions.

```text
short source-bounded counterfactual
>
long unsupported narrative
```

#### Counterfactual Path and Path Dependence

A Counterfactual Path may place pressure on a Path-Dependence claim by asking whether a source-supported alternative history would materially alter the tested present dimension. It does not by itself establish that the alternative outcome would have occurred, nor does it replace the Chapter-12 Current-State Sufficiency and Historical-Omission tests.

```text
counterfactual pressure
≠ Path-Dependence proof
```

Similarly, a Counterfactual Path may clarify the significance of a Lost Alternative or the selectivity of a realized Path. It does not prove that the unrealized continuation was preferable, successful, legitimate, or causally sufficient.

#### Counterfactual Path and general Counterfactual Sensitivity

Chapter 13 owns the source-bounded historical continuation used inside PATH. Chapter 46 retains the general system-wide Counterfactual-Sensitivity architecture for transformations and target claims. Chapter 13 therefore does not derive a universal counterfactual method or duplicate the later LIMITS treatment.

```text
Chapter-13 Counterfactual Path
≠ Chapter-46 general Counterfactual Sensitivity
```

#### C13-CF-01 — bounded counterfactual that stops before unsupported outcome

A public archive replaced two local catalogues with one central system. At the documented Branch Point, the archive had two funded and approved continuations:

1. immediate central cutover;
2. a six-month dual-running period with staged metadata reconciliation.

The archive selected immediate cutover. The staged plan contained staffing assignments, a reconciliation schedule, and a tested migration sequence. These materials support a Counterfactual Path through:

- continued operation of both catalogues for six months;
- staged transfer of the first two collections;
- two scheduled reconciliation reviews.

The sources do not establish how many conflicts the reviews would have found, whether users would have preferred the arrangement, whether the final cutover would have occurred on time, or whether later funding would have continued.

The warranted counterfactual therefore stops after the second scheduled review:

```text
source-supported staged entry
→ two documented transfer phases
→ second review
→ mandatory counterfactual horizon
```

The result is `admissible_with_bounded_claim`. Extending the path to “the staged plan would have prevented the later access crisis” would exceed the source ceiling and require claim reduction or stop.

<a id="13-10-non-selection-as-praxis-structure"></a>

### 13.10 Non-Selection as Praxis Structure

**Non-Selection** is the source-supported absence of selection among materially or conditionally available continuations inside an active decision context and a bounded temporal window, where that absence changes later reachability, cost, role, binding, residue, or Path formation.

```text
available alternatives
+ active selection context
+ bounded window
+ no selection
+ praxeological consequence
→ Non-Selection candidate
```

Non-Selection is not equivalent to missing information about whether a decision occurred. It also does not arise whenever no positive choice event is visible. The analyst must establish that a selection context existed and that the absence of selection carried structural consequences.

```text
missing decision record
≠ Non-Selection
```

#### Active decision context

An active decision context may be supported by:

- a formal review or authorization process;
- an announced deadline;
- a scheduled vote, tender, renewal, or response window;
- a role with a documented selection function;
- repeated requests requiring a bounded response;
- a rule under which silence, non-submission, or expiry selects a default continuation;
- a documented expectation that one of several continuations would be chosen.

The context does not require one sovereign chooser. It may be distributed across roles, committees, procedures, or sequential approvals. Chapter 13 reconstructs the selection architecture and its Path effects; it does not infer intention, motive, guilt, or personal responsibility.

#### Non-Selection, rejection, blockage, and deferral

Non-Selection remains distinct from nearby statuses:

- **Rejected Branch:** a continuation is source-supported as refused or not selected while available;
- **Blocked Branch:** an identifiable structure prevents entry or continuation;
- **Deferred Branch:** a continuation is shifted beyond its initial window while later candidacy remains;
- **Non-Selection:** the active selection window closes or persists without a warranted selection event, and that absence itself structures the Path.

One occurrence may later support more than one bounded description at different cuts. For example, repeated Non-Selection may defer a continuation and eventually contribute to its loss. These claims require separate temporal declarations; they must not be collapsed into one retrospective label.

#### Non-Selection and Λ

Non-Selection may be typed as a `Λ` occurrence only where the expectation frame, expected window, non-realization, source support, and praxeological load are warranted. Chapter 14 owns the full PATH-specific Non-Event analysis. Chapter 13 establishes only the selection-specific relation needed to reconstruct the alternative field.

```text
Non-Selection candidate
≠ Λ occurrence automatically
```

This boundary prevents Chapter 13 from treating every undecided matter, absent vote, unanswered proposal, or missing record as a structured Non-Event.

#### Non-Selection as implicit realization

Where a procedure specifies a default continuation, Non-Selection may realize that default without a positive selection event. The record must distinguish:

- the absence of selection;
- the rule or structure linking that absence to the default;
- the actual traversal of the default continuation.

```text
no positive choice event
+ bounded default rule
+ actual default traversal
→ possible implicit realization
```

The default's realization does not become rational, intended, legitimate, or inevitable merely because it followed procedurally.

#### C13-NSEL-01 — missed renewal window closes one continuation

A municipal data-sharing agreement required annual renewal by a joint committee. At the relevant cut, the committee had two materially available continuations:

1. renew the shared service for another year;
2. begin an already costed local replacement.

The committee scheduled three renewal meetings but did not reach a vote. The agreement expired automatically. The shared service then entered a six-month wind-down, while the local replacement had not been authorized. During the wind-down, two specialist roles were reassigned and the original replacement budget lapsed.

The record supports:

```text
active renewal context
+ two available continuations
+ repeated absence of selection
+ expiry default
+ later budget and role consequences
→ Non-Selection as Path-forming praxis structure
```

The case does not establish why individual committee members did not vote, whether renewal would have been preferable, or whether the local replacement would have succeeded. A bounded local mapping is `admissible_with_bounded_claim`.

<a id="13-11-alternative-space-compression"></a>

### 13.11 Alternative-Space Compression

**Alternative-Space Compression** is the reduction of the visible historical alternative field when a sequence, Path, Trajectory, case record, diagram, or later composition represents only selected continuations and branch relations.

Compression is unavoidable. A representation cannot preserve every proposal, micro-variation, tactical option, timing difference, and uncertain candidate. The methodological requirement is not exhaustive branching. It is disclosure of which alternatives materially carried the Path claim and what becomes less visible when the alternative space is reduced.

```text
necessary compression
≠ retrospective linearization
```

#### Why alternative space is compressed

Compression may be warranted to:

- preserve readable temporal structure;
- group minor variants into one continuation family;
- keep the claim within source and relevance boundaries;
- avoid multiplying unsupported or non-material branches;
- distinguish constitutive alternatives from illustrative detail;
- prepare a later `COMPOSE` record.

Compression becomes inadmissible when it removes the very alternative relation that makes a selection, blockage, delay, abortion, loss, or Path-Dependence claim intelligible.

#### Minimum preservation burden

A compressed alternative-space representation should preserve, where material:

- declared Branch Points;
- the realized continuation;
- source-supported rejected, blocked, aborted, deferred, or lost continuations;
- the temporal window of availability;
- the relation that differentiates the continuations;
- relevant Non-Selection;
- later reachability and residual effect;
- uncertainty about status or availability.

It may compress:

- minor variants inside one branch family;
- repeated equivalent proposals;
- local implementation options that do not alter the tested claim;
- uncertain micro-sequences below the Relevance Floor.

It may exclude:

- unsupported retrospective possibilities;
- alternatives outside the declared frame;
- logically possible but practically unavailable continuations;
- material irrelevant to the Path claim.

It must identify uncertainty and irrecoverability where source loss prevents reconstruction of the former alternative field.

#### Canonical Loss declaration

Alternative-Space Compression uses the existing five-part Loss structure:

```yaml
loss:
  preserved:
  compressed:
  excluded:
  uncertain:
  irrecoverable:
```

The branch statuses do not replace these fields. A Lost Alternative is a historical status claim; `irrecoverable` identifies information or structure that the representation or transformation cannot restore.

```text
historical alternative loss
≠ representation irrecoverability
```

#### Compression and graph rendering

A graph may display a highly legible fork while hiding uncertainty, source gaps, status differences, or later reconstitution. Conversely, it may display every imagined possibility and create a false impression of historical openness.

A responsible graph or Reader view must therefore distinguish at least:

- realized versus unrealized continuation;
- supported versus uncertain or unsupported status;
- historical window;
- continuation versus later reconstituted equivalent;
- visible branch versus compressed branch family;
- source-bounded counterfactual segment versus speculative continuation;
- stop or non-capture boundary.

```text
clean graph
≠ complete alternative space

many rendered branches
≠ historically open field
```

The Reader may improve TraceableLoad by letting users reveal compressed continuations, source pointers, uncertainty, and Loss. It may not infer branch status or upgrade the underlying claim.

#### C13-COMP-01 — compression preserves three material statuses and discloses omitted variants

A housing authority's redevelopment Path contained:

- one realized full-rebuild continuation;
- one rejected phased-renovation continuation;
- one blocked resident-led cooperative continuation;
- four minor financing variants within the phased-renovation family;
- two unsupported proposals mentioned only years later.

For the tested claim—why the realized Path closed local resident control—the compressed representation preserves the three material continuation families and the authorization barrier that blocked the cooperative route. It compresses the four financing variants into the phased-renovation family and excludes the two later unsupported proposals.

The Loss declaration states:

```yaml
loss:
  preserved:
    - realized rebuild continuation
    - rejected phased-renovation family
    - blocked cooperative continuation
    - historical window and authorization barrier
  compressed:
    - four financing variants inside phased renovation
  excluded:
    - two retrospective unsupported proposals
  uncertain:
    - internal ranking among the financing variants
  irrecoverable:
    - missing minutes from one preparatory meeting
```

This is an `admissible_with_bounded_claim` compression. A single line from “old estate” to “new estate” would erase the alternative structure and require claim reduction. A graph showing all nine named possibilities as equally available would exceed the Traceability Ceiling in the opposite direction.

<a id="13-12-alternative-status-record"></a>

### 13.12 Alternative Status Record

The **Alternative Status Record** is a bounded view inside the Shared Transformation Record for declaring the historical status, source basis, temporal window, later reachability, residual effect, uncertainty, and claim scope of one alternative continuation.

It is not a second Record system. It does not replace the required source, operation, target, admissibility, loss, alternatives, governance, or status declarations of the Shared Transformation Record.

```text
Alternative Status Record
= Shared-Record extension view
≠ parallel schema
≠ new operation
≠ historical truth engine
```

#### Schema ownership decision

The existing top-level `alternatives` field records rival transformations, rival decompositions, rival projections, no-transformation, non-translation, and unresolved analytical routes. It is not semantically identical to a historical branch alternative inside a Path.

```text
historical alternative
≠ rival STRATA transformation
```

WP3 therefore does **not** overload that field. It also does not add a new required top-level schema field. The existing controlled `extensions` carrier is sufficient because it already requires:

- an extension identifier;
- an owner or source;
- a control-source pointer;
- a declared purpose;
- a payload;
- confirmation that the extension does not replace a required field.

The Chapter-13 record view is carried as an owner-bound extension. No schema revision, parallel schema, new Rule, operation, Output Class, audit stage, status enum, score, probability, or truth engine is required.

#### Canonical extension form

```yaml
extensions:
  - extension_id: pms-strata:chapter13:alternative-status:v0.1
    owner_or_source: PMS-STRATA Chapter 13
    control_source_pointer: 01_blocks/02_part_i_path.md#13-12-alternative-status-record
    purpose: Declare one source-bounded historical continuation without replacing required Shared-Record fields.
    payload:
      alternative_status:
        reference_path:
        branch_point:
        historical_window:
        candidate_continuation:
        availability_status:
        source_support:
        selection_status:
        blocking_structure:
        initiation_trace:
        deferral_structure:
        later_reachability:
        residual_effect:
        uncertainty:
        claim_scope:
        output_class_mapping:
    does_not_replace_required_field: true
```

One extension entry should normally represent one candidate continuation. Several entries may share a Branch Point where multiple alternatives require separate status and evidence declarations. Grouping several continuations into one payload is permissible only where their source basis, status, and uncertainty remain individually inspectable.

#### Field burdens

`reference_path` identifies the realized or candidate Path relative to which the alternative is reconstructed.

`branch_point` identifies the historical configuration or cut at which the continuation became selectable, contestable, blocked, deferred, initiated, or later lost.

`historical_window` declares when the continuation was available, conditionally available, prepared, contested, or otherwise relevant.

`candidate_continuation` names the continuation without treating the name as proof of identity or availability.

`availability_status` records the bounded local finding, such as materially available, conditionally available, prepared, contested, uncertain, or unsupported. These are not canonical Output Classes.

`source_support` distinguishes direct support, indirect reconstruction, negative evidence, source gaps, and rival interpretations.

`selection_status` may declare realized, rejected, blocked, aborted, deferred, lost, unresolved, or not established, while preserving status/cause separation and cut-specific change.

`blocking_structure`, `initiation_trace`, and `deferral_structure` are populated only where relevant. Empty or not-applicable fields must not be used to imply a negative finding.

`later_reachability` declares whether the continuation remained reachable, reopened, was reconstituted under changed conditions, became materially unreachable, or remains uncertain.

`residual_effect` records costs, expectations, infrastructure, bindings, exclusions, evidence burdens, or later Path effects left by the unrealized or interrupted continuation.

`uncertainty` preserves unresolved availability, status, timing, causal mechanism, continuity, and source limitations.

`claim_scope` limits the alternative claim by object, frame, temporal window, role, dimension, and generalization ceiling.

`output_class_mapping` maps the local result to one of the ten canonical Output Classes. It does not create a branch-status Output Class.

#### Record completeness does not establish historical status

A syntactically complete extension can still contain unsupported, overbroad, or contradictory claims. Formal validation may check that fields were supplied and that the extension does not replace required fields. It cannot determine that an alternative was historically available, rejected, blocked, aborted, deferred, lost, or causally consequential.

```text
record completeness
≠ availability proof
≠ status proof
```

#### C13-REC-01 — complete record with bounded unresolved status

A university considered maintaining a regional field station, relocating it, or closing it. The extension records the relocation continuation with a two-year historical window, direct evidence of preliminary site review, indirect evidence of available funding, no initiation trace, conflicting minutes about whether the governing board rejected relocation or whether land access blocked it, and uncertain later reachability.

The record is complete, but the local finding remains:

```text
availability: conditionally supported
selection status: rejected_or_blocked_unresolved
output class: non_capture
```

Completeness improves inspection. It does not resolve the historical ambiguity.

#### Failure and Claim Reduction

An alternative claim fails or requires reduction where:

- the historical cut or window is absent;
- later possibility is substituted for earlier availability;
- the continuation was merely imaginable;
- source support establishes the realized Path but not the alternative field;
- rejection, blockage, abortion, deferral, or loss is asserted without its positive burden;
- a Counterfactual Path continues beyond the source ceiling;
- Non-Selection is inferred from missing records without an active decision context;
- compression hides a constitutive alternative or status uncertainty;
- a later equivalent is treated as continuous restoration;
- a complete record is treated as proof.

The preferred reduction route is:

```text
strong branch-status claim
→ bounded candidate continuation
→ uncertain historical alternative
→ realized Path without established alternative field
→ no alternative claim
```

The weaker warranted Path, Transition, source-gap, or uncertainty finding remains available.

##### C13-REDUCE-01 — claimed Lost Alternative reduced to current unavailability

A report states that a cross-border rail route is a “lost alternative” because present regulations prohibit it. The only historical source is a later interview saying that the route “might once have been considered.” No contemporaneous plan, permission, budget, technical review, or decision window survives.

The sources support current unavailability and retrospective possibility language. They do not support earlier practical availability.

```text
claimed Lost Alternative
→ earlier availability unsupported
→ reduce to current unavailability plus uncertain retrospective candidate
```

The appropriate mapping is `claim_reduction_required`, not Lost Alternative.

#### Mandatory Stop

`mandatory_stop` applies where an analyst knowingly continues an unsupported alternative or Counterfactual Path in order to:

- assign blame, legitimacy, rationality, or person-level judgment;
- claim that an unrealized continuation would have succeeded;
- predict future behavior or outcomes;
